#### Footer audio lifted into a root-layout sound provider

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | [#30](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/30) |
| **Files** | `src/components/sound/SoundProvider.jsx`, `src/components/sound/FloatingSoundToggle.jsx`, `src/app/layout.js`, `src/components/footer/index.jsx` |
| **Details** | **Persistent footer audio** ([#30](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/30), `src/components/sound/SoundProvider.jsx`). The footer guitar track's `<audio>` element and on/off state moved out of `Footer` into a `SoundProvider` mounted in the **root** layout. `Footer` is rendered by the `(sub pages)` layout, which **unmounts on navigation to `/`** — that was destroying the audio node mid-play and cutting the music off. The track now survives route changes; the footer renders the same visible control but reads its state from context (`useSound`). A new `FloatingSoundToggle` gives footerless routes (the homepage) an always-available stop control, rendering `null` where a footer already carries one. The purely-visual overlays (Now Playing, toaster, cursor) render **outside** the provider, so a toggle can't re-render them. |

#### Architect & contact-intro copy darkens gold→ember in reading order

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | [#87](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/87) |
| **Files** | `src/lib/fireRamp.js`, `src/components/about/index.jsx`, `src/components/contact/ContactIntro.jsx` |
| **Details** | **Reading-order fire fill** ([#87](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/87), `src/lib/fireRamp.js`). The About "Architect of Enchantment" paragraph and the contact-page intro clipped an **identical** per-word vertical gold→ember gradient to every word, so the copy read as one flat colour band left-to-right. A new shared helper `wordFill(i, total)` samples the `.text-fire-amber` ramp at each word's position and slides its sheen window along it, so the paragraph now darkens from bright gold (first words) to deep ember (last words) **in reading order** — restoring the whole-paragraph gradient that per-word clipping (the earlier GPU-compositing fix) had flattened, without reintroducing the clip-once-per-layer bug: each word still owns its clip. `rampColor(t)` linearly interpolates the ramp and both paragraphs stay in sync via the single helper (mirroring the `.text-fire-amber` stops). The first word's top edge is still exactly `#ffd27d`, so the opening reads unchanged — only the tail darkens. |

#### Qualifications image sizing, formats & cache TTL

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | [#84](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/84) |
| **Files** | `next.config.mjs`, `src/components/qualifications/certSizes.js`, `src/components/qualifications/Carousel.jsx` |
| **Details** | **Image optimiser tuning** ([#84](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/84), `next.config.mjs`). The optimiser now sets a one-year `minimumCacheTTL` — immutable certificate assets stop sending an `If-Modified-Since` revalidation on every navigation — and prefers AVIF over WebP. It deliberately keeps Next's **default** `deviceSizes` (no global cap): `images.*` is site-wide, and a cap would clamp every full-bleed `sizes="100vw"` background (home / about / projects) to 1200 px and soften it on large / high-DPR displays. Instead the carousel self-limits **at the source** — a new shared `certSizes.js` derives each card's responsive `sizes` from its *true* height-bound rendered width (`min(--cert-w-cap, aspectRatio × --cert-cap)`), so the browser targets ~1080/1200 for certificates on their own merit while every other image keeps its full variant set (verified: cards resolve to `w=640`/`1080` while the home background stays `w=1920` on desktop). `certSizes` is the single source of truth imported by **both** the carousel `<Image>` and the preloader, so their requested widths — and therefore the cache-hit contract — can't drift apart. |

#### Homepage hero cohesion pass — the scene pushed back into being an environment

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (owner-directed: the causeway was competing with the UI rather than hosting it) |
| **Files** | `src/app/globals.css`, `scripts/scene/bake-home-grade.mjs`, `public/background/home-hero.webp`, `assets/source/home-hero-src.webp` |
| **Details** | The production hero worked because its background was near-black and empty, so the neon-orange UI owned the frame outright. The causeway is a **co-star**: bright teal, perspective lines converging directly behind the laptop, and two foreground lanterns sitting at exactly the nav ring's height. Nothing was wrong with either half — they were competing for the same job. Four changes, each removing one specific competitor. **`.home-scrim`** gains a *stage well* (a soft dark ellipse under the laptop/nav cluster, centred at 62% because the cluster sits below the frame's middle) and an *edge vignette* (transparent across the middle 55%, closing hard into the corners). The well is an ellipse rather than a raised floor deliberately: the scrim's centre band was the LIGHTEST on purpose so the vanishing-point mist could rim-light the laptop, and darkening the centre uniformly would trade the composition's best feature for its noisiest. The vignette is also what dims the two lanterns fighting the buttons — by their POSITION in frame, so the causeway's receding lights keep their full run to the vanishing point. **`.custom-bg`** gets a real substrate: its background was `rgb(var(--background) / 0.2)`, which over the old black plate was invisible *and* harmless, but over a photograph leaves white glyphs sitting on the causeway's own lanterns and mist — the brightest, busiest part of the frame. Now a mostly-opaque dark plate with a radial falloff (densest under the glyph, easing at the rim so it still reads as a lens, not a coin) plus `saturate(0.75)` in the backdrop filter. **The plate is graded** (`scripts/scene/bake-home-grade.mjs`, baked because the shader resamples this exact file — a CSS filter would grade still and moving layers inconsistently — and because a full-viewport filter is a permanent per-frame cost on the LCP route). Saturation 0.80 with only a nudge cooler: desaturating a blue image necessarily *warms* it (chroma pulled toward luminance raises the lowest channel, here red), so "cooler" and "less saturated" pull against each other, and the first cut had to cut red 16% to win — which would have taken the lantern flames down with it. Global darkening was also dropped after it clipped highlights 255→245: the scrim's new well and vignette darken by *region*, which is the better tool anyway. The original is kept as `home-hero-src.webp` and the script always grades from it, so the bake is re-cuttable and reversible and can never compound. **`.laptop-contact`** deepens 0.62→0.86 with a tighter core and wider skirt: a real contact shadow on wet stone has near-total occlusion directly under the object, and at the old level over lantern-lit stone it read as a smudge rather than as contact. |


#### Homepage arrival ignite burns the headline's colour — the sweep and its bloom off warm yellow

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (owner-directed: "the yellowish colour that sweeps the bridge and ends in a boom") |
| **Files** | `src/components/home/HomePathIgnite.jsx` |
| **Details** | `HomePathIgnite` paints three fills additively (`globalCompositeOperation = 'lighter'`), and two of them — the wavefront travelling the causeway, and the bloom that lands at the vanishing point — carried a **temperature ramp** rather than a colour: near-white core `rgba(255,246,214)` → warm yellow `rgba(252,214,140)` → amber edge `rgba(234,181,62)`. Additive stacking of a near-white hot end is exactly what makes a light read *yellow* on a dark plate, whatever the mid stop says. Both ramps now hold one hue — `rgba(255, 109, 5, α)`, the `#ff6d05` the `MUHAMMAD ABDULLAH` `h1` is stroked in — with alpha alone carrying the falloff, the same way the ripple rings, their launch bloom and the laptop's hover shadow were written in the cohesion pass above. **Nothing else changed**: every alpha coefficient, radius, easing curve and timing constant (`TRAVEL_MS` 1700, `BLOOM_MS` 800, `DECAY`, `RISE`) is byte-identical, so the gesture is the one already tuned — only its colour moved. **The lanterns catching are deliberately left alone.** Their flare is the plate's own photographed fire re-flaring, and it decays back into `HomeSceneGlow`'s ambient `rgba(255,228,186)` halo and the filmed flame loop; recolouring it would pop orange and settle yellow, which reads as a defect rather than a decision. The cold mist breath pushed out with the bloom (`rgba(150,198,236)`) also stays — it exists to be the counterpoint to the warm light, not part of it. |


#### Scene asset scripts collected into `scripts/scene/` — and made runnable again

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (owner-directed working-tree audit: "check if all 94 files are needed, and what has gone wrong that some are in the main root") |
| **Files** | **moved** 24 scripts from the repo root into `scripts/scene/` (the six `.`-prefixed ones un-hidden: `.ghost-hunt` / `.hero-crop-audit` / `.measure-flames` / `.motion-check` / `.scene-verify` / `.swap-check`) · **new** `scripts/scene/workdir.mjs`, `scripts/scene/README.md` · **moved** `public/background/home-hero-src.webp` → `assets/source/home-hero-src.webp` · **removed** `.flame-strip.mjs`, `.ghost-extent.mjs`, `gen-home-video.mjs` · `.gitignore`, `package.json`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | The scene pipeline had accumulated **27 loose scripts at the repository root**, which is where they were authored (run from the root, output beside them) and where they stayed. That was untidy; the real defect underneath it was not. **19 of the 27 hard-coded an absolute path into a per-session agent scratchpad** — `/private/tmp/claude-501/…/<uuid>/scratchpad` — and every one of those directories was long gone, so a script the README cites as the provenance of a shipped asset could not be re-run by anyone, on any machine. Four more spawned an `ffmpeg` binary from inside one of those dead scratchpads, even though `ffmpeg-static` is a devDependency of this repo. **The fix is one contract, not 19 edits**: `workdir.mjs` exports `WORK = process.env.SCRATCH ?? './.scene-work'` and creates the directory on import — created rather than merely resolved, because several of these scripts write their first output minutes into a decode, and a missing directory should not be discovered there. `.scene-work/` is gitignored; the four ffmpeg call sites now import `ffmpeg-static` and keep their `FFMPEG=` override. **The two shell scripts were outside that contract in both halves, and both mattered.** `finish-projects-scene.sh` staged everything in `mktemp -d` under `TMPDIR` and deleted it on exit — but its near-lossless master is the INPUT to the loop search, the loop assembly and the poster, so re-cutting at a different `FADE` or re-checking a seam meant paying for a full re-grade of a 2560×1440 clip, and nothing was left to inspect when a result looked wrong; `bake-table-flames.sh` kept `${TMPDIR:-/tmp}/table-flames`, which additionally held **the only copy of the pre-bake asset ladder** it backs up before overwriting `public/background/`. Both now take a named subdirectory of the same `SCRATCH` work dir (`projects-scene/`, `table-flames/`) with fixed filenames, so reruns overwrite instead of accumulating and nothing survives only in a directory the OS may sweep. Both also resolved ffmpeg from `PATH` (`${FFMPEG:-ffmpeg}`, and a `command -v ffmpeg || exit 1` guard) on a machine that **has no system ffmpeg** — so `bake-table-flames.sh` could not run at all — and now resolve the `ffmpeg-static` binary themselves, keeping `FFMPEG=` as the override. Verified by evaluating each script's prelude from three working directories: both resolve the same absolute work dir and the same executable ffmpeg from the repo root, from `scripts/scene/`, and from `/tmp`, and `SCRATCH=` relocates both. **The documented `FFMPEG=` override was then made true everywhere**: five `.mjs` (`bake-causeway-water`, `ghost-hunt`, `remask-causeway-posts`, `scene-verify`, `swap-check`) imported the `ffmpeg-static` binary and passed it straight to `execFileSync`, so the override the README promised silently did nothing in 5 of the 9 scripts that run ffmpeg — aliasing it at the import (`const ffmpeg = process.env.FFMPEG ?? ffmpegStatic`) fixes all five without touching a single call site. Resolution is now identical in all 11: `FFMPEG=` if set, else the bundled binary, and **never `PATH`** — a PATH fallback sounds forgiving but its real effect is that some machines silently encode with a different ffmpeg build than the one the measured seam and QP settings were tuned against. Proved by pointing `FFMPEG=` at a decoy binary and watching both the shell and Node resolution paths pick it up, and by re-checking that no `${FFMPEG:-ffmpeg}`, `command -v ffmpeg`, or literal `'ffmpeg'` spawn survives anywhere in the directory. **One script did not survive the move untouched, and it is the interesting one.** No `.mjs` resolves anything relative to its own file (no `import.meta.url` / `__dirname` anywhere) — every path is repo-root-relative, e.g. `src/components/home/homeSceneLights.js` and `public/background/…` — so those behave identically provided they are still invoked from the root, which every usage comment and both shell scripts' internal sibling calls now say explicitly. But `finish-projects-scene.sh` derived `DIR` from `$0` and used that **one** anchor for two different jobs: locating the sibling `.mjs` files it drives (correct) *and* locating the repo's `public/background/` output (correct only while the script sat at the repo root). From `scripts/scene/` it would have written the finished loop and its poster to `scripts/scene/public/background/` — failing on the missing directory, or worse, silently producing assets the app never loads. The two anchors are now separate (`DIR` for siblings, `ROOT="${DIR}/../.."` for outputs), which as a side effect makes that script correct from **any** working directory, since the two `.mjs` it calls take explicit paths and read nothing cwd-relative. Verified by resolving all 21 repo paths referenced across all 24 scripts (0 unresolvable), `bash -n` on both shell scripts, `node --check` on all 22 `.mjs`, and re-running `detect-lights.mjs` end to end from its new home. Verified end to end: all 22 `.mjs` parse, and `node scripts/scene/detect-lights.mjs` re-runs from the new location, re-measuring the lantern rig off the shipped plate and writing its annotated preview into the new work dir. **Three scripts were deleted outright** — `.flame-strip.mjs` and `.ghost-extent.mjs` (throwaway comparison probes, pinned to dead scratchpads, cited nowhere) and `gen-home-video.mjs` (superseded by `gen-causeway-video.mjs`; the homepage ships no full-scene video). **`home-hero-src.webp` left `public/`**: it is the ungraded plate `bake-home-grade.mjs` grades *from*, deliberately kept so the bake can never compound — but a build input sitting in `public/` is uploaded, deployed and publicly served on every request path that guesses its name, for 264 KB nobody fetches. It now lives in `assets/source/`, with the three scripts that read it repointed. Also swept: `package-lock.json` was listed in `.gitignore` while being **tracked**, so the rule was a no-op that only misled anyone reading it (lockfiles belong in the repo — the rule was the wrong half); and `@eslint/js` + `globals` were dropped from devDependencies, orphaned when the inert `eslint.config.mjs` went (ESLint 8.57 does not read flat config without `ESLINT_USE_FLAT_CONFIG`, so `.eslintrc.json` was always the live config — `npm run lint` verified unchanged before and after). |


### Fixed

#### Command palette — the search combobox has an accessible name of its own

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the palette's combobox had no programmatic accessible name. The dialog's `aria-label` names the dialog, not the nested input, and a placeholder must not stand in for a persistent label. Asked for an explicit label such as `aria-label="Search commands"`. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/commandPaletteLabel.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/commandPalette/CommandPalette.jsx`, `tests/unit/commandPaletteScroll.test.js` |
| **Details** | The input carries `aria-label="Search commands"`, with a comment saying why neither the dialog's label nor the placeholder could do the job: a dialog's name does not reach a nested control, and a placeholder is a hint that vanishes on the first keystroke. The placeholder stays as that hint; the listbox keeps its own "Commands" name. **Tests** — a new case opens the palette and reads the accessible name the way assistive tech would (RTL's `name` computation): the combobox is "Search commands", the dialog is still "Command palette", the placeholder is present but is not the name, and the controlled listbox is "Commands". The scroll suite now finds the input by that name, so every keyboard case there holds the label too. |

#### `/guestbook` motion toggle — one switch reaches every layer: framer, the aurora, and the CSS shimmer

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the palette's "Toggle motion" only affected Framer Motion, through `MotionConfig`. `AuroraDustMount` read the OS media query directly, and guestbook CSS animations such as `.gb-pending` were gated only by that query, so selecting motion off still left visible animation running. Asked to propagate the setting to the non-Framer layers and expose a class or data attribute that disables the CSS animations too. |
| **Files** | **new** `src/hooks/useReducedMotion.js`, `tests/unit/guestbookMotionToggle.test.js`, `tests/unit/auroraDustMount.test.js`; **changed** `src/app/(sub pages)/guestbook/page.js`, `src/components/AuroraDustMount.jsx`, `src/app/globals.css`, `vitest.config.mjs`, `README.md`, and the `useReducedMotion` import in the sixteen files of the page's tree — `src/components/guestbook/{GuestbookTitle,GuestbookWall,MessageCard,MessageInput,PresencePill,ReactionBar,SignInPrompt,SignatureField,SignatureGlyph,WallPagination}.jsx`, `src/components/commandPalette/CommandPalette.jsx`, `src/components/PageTitle.jsx`, `src/components/contact/MessageRefine.jsx`, `src/hooks/{useCountUp,useMagneticPull,useCardTilt}.js` |
| **Details** | **The deeper half, found by the new test.** The page's own comment claimed `MotionConfig reducedMotion="always"` reached "every framer animation — they all read `useReducedMotion`". It did not: framer's `useReducedMotion` reads the OS media query alone, and `MotionConfig` reaches only `motion.*` props by another path. So every `reduceMotion ? … : …` branch in the tree — the presence ping, the ember burst, the count-ups, the signature draw-on, the panel slides, the tilt and magnet hooks — ignored the toggle entirely; the probe in the new suite read `false` under `"always"`. A project `useReducedMotion` (`src/hooks/useReducedMotion.js`) now answers "the OS preference, or `always` from a `MotionConfig` above" — a strict extension of today's behaviour, and deliberately NOT framer's `useReducedMotionConfig`, which under the default context (`"never"`) answers `false` whatever the OS says and would have silenced the OS preference on every page without a provider. The sixteen files in the guestbook page's tree import it in place of framer's; the rest of the site's framer imports are equivalent today, since no other page mounts a `MotionConfig`. **One verdict, four carriers.** The page still wraps everything in `MotionConfig reducedMotion="always"` when the pref is off — for `motion.*` props, and now for every component branch through the hook. It also wraps the content in a `display: contents` div carrying `data-motion="on|off"` — the element exists for its attribute and generates no box, so the fixed layers, the layout and the one `<main>` landmark are untouched — and hands the same pref to the aurora mount as a new `enabled` prop (default `true`, so /about, /contact, /journey, /my-past and the 404 behave exactly as before). **The mount honours both.** `AuroraDustMount` unmounts the canvas when `enabled` is false exactly as it does under the OS query, the static image showing either way; the query still rules whenever a page says nothing. **CSS gates twice.** `globals.css` gains `[data-motion='off'] .gb-pending::after { animation: none; opacity: 0 }` beside the existing `prefers-reduced-motion` block, with a note that every guestbook keyframe must carry both gates — a media query cannot see a preference the visitor set in the palette. Colour hovers (`transition-colors`, the fire-word flip) are left as they are, as they are under the OS preference: they are not motion. SSR stays honest: the attribute follows the store's server snapshot ("on") and flips after hydration through `useSyncExternalStore`, as `MotionConfig` already did. **Tests** — a jsdom suite renders the real page through the real preference store with the heavy children replaced by probes: with motion on, the project hook inside the app slot reads false, the aurora receives `enabled=true` and the wrapper says `on`; one `togglePref('motion')` flips all three at once — while framer's raw hook, read beside it, still says false, which is the finding — and another flips them back; the wrapper carries `contents`. Importing a Next page (JSX in a `.js` file) needed `vitest.config.mjs` to widen Vite 8's oxc transform: `src/**/*.js` added to its include, the default `.js` exclusion lifted, and the language pinned to `jsx` — honoured at runtime ahead of the extension — which is safe because the repo is JavaScript-only (the deprecated `esbuild` key never reaches the filter). Its CSS half is a source contract on `globals.css`: the shimmer runs by default, the OS block stills it, and the `[data-motion='off']` rule stills it too. A second suite drives the real `AuroraDustMount` with the Three canvas swapped for a marker through `next/dynamic` and the loader told to have lifted: it mounts by default, stays unmounted for `enabled={false}` whatever the OS says, mounts and unmounts as the prop flips, and stays unmounted under the OS preference whether the page says nothing or says yes. |

#### `/guestbook` poll — a burst of arrivals can no longer leave a hole in the wall

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: a poll could silently lose arrivals when more than `PAGE_SIZE` messages landed between polls. The fetched page held only the newest eight, `mergeNewestPage` kept the old local tail beneath it, so the ninth — between those ranges — was absent; and because the hook kept its older continuation cursor, no later fetch could recover it. Asked to poll until the new page overlaps the prefix, or reset the cursor to the page and refill before preserving the tail. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/useGuestbookMessagesPoll.test.js`; **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/paging.js`, `src/hooks/useGuestbookMessages.js`, `tests/unit/paging.test.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **Both, in that order.** `paging.js` gains `pageReachesPrefix(page, prev, limit)`: the pure question the poll asks before merging — a short page reaches (the server holds nothing older to miss); so does a prefix with nothing confirmed at or below the page's top, a shared id, or a page whose oldest is no newer than the prefix's top-at-or-below-the-page (its position lies inside the page's range, present or deleted since). A FULL page entirely above that card does not — that is the hole. The one subtlety is a local card newer than the page's top, our own post confirmed after the poll's request was cut: it sits above the page and is not a join point, so the join is judged against the prefix beneath it. **The poll follows the page down.** While the run of pages fetched does not reach the prefix and the server has more, the poll reads the next page from the run's own cursor — at most `POLL_MAX_FETCHES` (5) requests, forty arrivals in one interval, which the one-post-per-user-per-five-minutes limit makes a forty-author burst — then merges the whole run with `mergeNewestPage`, keeping the old tail and the prefix's own cursor exactly as before when the tail survives, and taking the run's cursor when it does not (or when the server says there is nothing older at all). **Past the bound, it restarts.** A prefix with a hole is the one thing never kept: the list becomes the pending cards plus the contiguous run just fetched, and the cursor becomes the run's own, so the older leaves refill from there as the visitor pages to them — cards re-fetched, never cards missing. Arrivals across the whole run glow as new; the "only once there is a wall" guard is unchanged. **Tests** — six pure cases pin `pageReachesPrefix`, including the deleted-since and own-post-above-the-page shapes. A new hook suite drives the real poll (triggered the way the page does, on a tab coming back) through a cursor-paged stand-in wall: three arrivals are one read merged above the kept tail; nine arrivals are exactly two reads and the ninth is present, with the prefix still ending at the wall's oldest; a prefix deeper than the walk keeps its tail and its own cursor, and a page flip continues from there; fifty arrivals are five reads, then a restart holding exactly the wall's top forty with no tail beneath, refilled contiguously by `ensureLoaded` and `loadUntil` down to the oldest; and a pending card rides on top through a walk and through a restart, counted once. |

#### `/guestbook/presence` — the unmetered read-only GET is gone; every Redis touch now sits behind the heartbeat limiter

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the anonymous `GET` on `/api/guestbook/presence` bypassed the presence limiter, yet `presenceCount()` still ran a Redis `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE` plus `ZCARD` on every call. The application heartbeat uses `POST` exclusively, so the GET was a way to generate unbounded Redis traffic despite the route's stated abuse ceiling. Asked to remove the unused handler or put an IP-based read limit in front of it. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/app/api/guestbook/presence/route.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/presence.js`, `tests/unit/presenceRoute.test.js` |
| **Details** | **Removed, not metered.** Nothing called the GET: `usePresence` only ever POSTs (the heartbeat's reply is its read — register and poll are one round-trip), the e2e stub is method-agnostic, and the README never promised a GET. A read nobody needs is not worth a second limiter key, so the handler is deleted — Next answers a method the route file does not export with `405`, before any handler runs — and `presenceCount()` goes with it, since the route was its only consumer. The presence store's whole surface is now `heartbeat()`, which the route calls only after the per-IP budget has admitted the beat, so every Redis command the module can issue is behind the limiter. The route and store headers say so. **Tests** — the flood case, which used the GET to read the count after the refused beat, now reads it the way the product does: a second client's heartbeat answers with the budget plus one, proving the refused id was never registered (plus two would mean it had been). The former "another client is still served" case is folded into that assertion, and the suite pins that the route module exports no `GET`. |

#### Command palette hook suite — every test unmounts its hook, so one ⌘K reaches one listener

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `useCommandPalette.test.js` never unmounted its `renderHook` results. With Vitest globals disabled, React Testing Library registers no automatic cleanup (as `commandPaletteScroll.test.js` already notes), so each test left a `window` keydown listener alive and every later hotkey also toggled the hooks of earlier tests, making the focus assertions order-dependent. Asked for `cleanup()` before the body is cleared. |
| **Files** | **changed** `tests/unit/useCommandPalette.test.js` |
| **Details** | `afterEach` now imports RTL's `cleanup` and calls it first, then clears the focusables — the scroll suite's explicit-unmount pattern, with a comment saying why: without globals nothing unmounts a hook, and a leftover hook is a live `window` keydown subscription whose `close()` re-targets focus on the next test's ⌘K. The leak was latent rather than failing — the stale hooks' focus targets were already detached by the body clear, so their `focus()` calls were no-ops — which is why the suite passed under `--sequence.shuffle` before the change too; the fix removes the dependence rather than a red run. Verified: the three cases pass in file order and shuffled across several seeds, and the full unit suite is green (28 files, 203 tests). |

#### JSON store suite — the two-entry directory assertion no longer trusts `readdir` order

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `store.test.js` compared `readdir(dir)` directly against `['blocker', 'guestbook.json']`. `readdir` does not guarantee entry order — APFS and ext4 both return hashed B-tree order — so the one assertion that lists two entries could fail intermittently by filesystem; the single-entry assertions were already safe. |
| **Files** | **changed** `tests/unit/store.test.js` |
| **Details** | The two-entry listing is sorted before the comparison, with a comment saying why; the single-entry assertions are untouched. The store suite and the full unit suite pass. |

#### `/guestbook` identity — authors are keyed by the OAuth account id; the GitHub login is display data only

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `profile.login` was persisted as the author key and drove ownership, rate limiting and reactions. GitHub accounts can be renamed, so a rename made an author's prior messages no longer theirs and gave them a fresh reaction and rate-limit identity — and a released login could be claimed by someone else, who would inherit all three. Asked for a provider-prefixed immutable account id (`account.providerAccountId`) as the persisted key, with the login kept as separate display data. A follow-up finding on the first cut then ruled the login out of the moderator setting as well: keeping it as a fallback for `GUESTBOOK_ADMIN` re-opened the same takeover for delete-any, so moderation now requires the key exclusively. |
| **Files** | **new** `src/lib/guestbook/identity.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/sessionCallbacks.js`, `tests/unit/identity.test.js`, `tests/unit/sessionCallbacks.test.js`; **changed** `src/auth.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/admin.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/ratelimit.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/redisDriver.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/jsonDriver.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/store.js`, `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js`, `src/app/api/guestbook/reactions/route.js`, `src/hooks/useGuestbookMessages.js` (comment), `tests/unit/guestbookRoute.test.js`, `tests/unit/reactionsRoute.test.js`, `tests/unit/ratelimit.test.js`, `tests/unit/admin.test.js`, `tests/unit/redisDriver.test.js`, `tests/e2e/guestbook-composer.spec.js`, `.env.example`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **One key, minted once.** `identity.js` builds `key = provider:providerAccountId` in the Auth.js `jwt` callback — `github:<numeric id>` / `google:<sub>` — and the session carries it; `viewerFromSession()` is the only way a route reads identity. The stored author is `{ name, username?, avatar, provider, key }`: `key` is what `ownsMessage()` compares for `isOwn` and DELETE, what both limiters (`checkRateLimit`, `checkReactionRateLimit`) meter, and the field of the reactions hash; `username` is the GitHub login, present and shipped only for GitHub authors, never compared. Google's key is byte-identical to the `google:<sub>` username the wall stored before, so `authorKey()` reads that legacy form back and those rows stay owned; legacy GitHub rows (login, no key) are owned by nobody — a deliberate cut rather than a login fallback that would re-open the hole for exactly those rows; the moderator can still remove them. **Admin, key only.** `GUESTBOOK_ADMIN` must hold the owner's identity key `github:<providerAccountId>`; `isAdminIdentity()` compares it exactly with the viewer's key and never reads the login. A bare username — the pre-key form — grants nobody and logs one warning naming the fix, so a misconfiguration fails closed and visibly instead of handing delete-any to whoever later claims a renamed owner's old login. The Auth.js callbacks moved out of `auth.js` into `sessionCallbacks.js` as pure functions over the shapes Auth.js passes, so what a sign-in writes to the JWT and what a session read copies from it are unit-tested without booting next-auth. **Sessions.** A JWT minted before keys existed has no key and is not upgraded (a login cannot be turned back into an id without a GitHub API call): its writes answer 401 until the person signs in again; the wall is unreleased, so this touches preview sessions only. **Wire.** No guestbook response carries a key — not another visitor's, not the viewer's own; the route tests assert against the raw text of every response, a signed-in GET included. The one place a key does reach a browser is its owner's own session payload (`/api/auth/session`, which `SessionProvider` reads): Auth.js builds one session object for both `auth()` on the server and that endpoint, the routes derive identity from it through `viewerFromSession()`, and the client never reads it — acceptable because it is the person's own account id (a GitHub id is public on the GitHub API; a Google sub is their own app-scoped id). A review pass caught the README claiming the key "never leaves the server"; the guarantee is now stated as it actually holds, here and in `identity.js`, `sessionCallbacks.js` and `route.js`, rather than adding a JWT-decoding identity path to the routes with nothing to protect. **Tests** — `identity.test.js` covers minting (a renamed GitHub account keeps its key and only its handle moves; Google gets no username; an unkeyable account mints nothing), `viewerFromSession` (a legacy token is null), and `ownsMessage` (key match whatever the login, never a login match, exact case, legacy Google row owned, legacy GitHub row owned by nobody). The route suite adds the rename scenario end-to-end: a message posted as `before-rename` is still `isOwn` and deletable after the rename, while a different account holding the old login sees `isOwn: false` and a 403; a keyless session gets 401 on POST and DELETE. The reactions suite files the reaction under the key; the limiter suite proves the login is never compared and the legacy Google form still is; the admin suite covers the exact key match (the renamed owner still the moderator, the old login's new holder never), the refused login form and its one-time warning; the session-callback suite covers what sign-in mints (a GitHub login as display data, a Google token with none, a stale username cleared), what a session read copies (`isAdmin` true only for the key-form match, rotation taking effect on the next read) and a legacy keyless token stamping no identity. |

#### `/guestbook` legacy identities — an operator-run migration backfills keys from an authoritative login → account-id mapping

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the wall and its `guestbook:*` Redis namespace predate identity keys, so deployed storage can hold rows written under the login model. A legacy GitHub message carries only a login — `authorKey()` answers null, so `ownsMessage()` refuses the actual author (`isOwn: false`, DELETE `403`) — and a legacy reaction field stays filed under the login when the same person's next reaction lands under `github:<id>`: the count includes both and `viewerReaction` reads null until they react again. Asked for a migration over an authoritative login-to-account mapping that backfills `author.key` and rewrites the legacy reaction fields atomically, without restoring username-based authorization. |
| **Files** | **new** `src/lib/guestbook/legacyIdentity.js`, `scripts/guestbook-migrate-identity.mjs`, `tests/unit/legacyIdentity.test.js`; **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/identity.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/redisDriver.js`, `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js` (comments), `README.md` |
| **Details** | **The plan is pure; the write is atomic.** `legacyIdentity.js` classifies every stored author by the fields it carries (`keyed`, `legacy-github` — a bare login and no key, `legacy-keyed` — the pre-key Google form whose username *is* the key, `anonymous`, `unknown`) and plans the repair: a legacy GitHub author gets `key = github:<id>` **only** from the mapping and otherwise stays owned by nobody; a legacy Google author's `google:<sub>` username moves into `key` and is dropped from the row (route.js already stripped it on the wire). Reaction maps fold by identity — a bare-login field moves under the key, and where the key already holds a value the person reacted again after keys arrived, so that newer choice stays and the legacy field is dropped; counts are one per person again. **Two admissible sources, neither a lookup of who holds the login today** (the rename hazard `identity.js` rules out): the operator's explicit pairs (`--map login=id`, `--mapping file.json`; validated against GitHub's login grammar and refused on a typo rather than mapping the wrong person, one login given two ids refused too), and what the wall itself recorded at write time (`--derive`): a legacy author's `avatar` is the OAuth profile's `avatar_url`, whose `/u/<id>` path segment is the poster's numeric id as GitHub served it at POST time, and a keyed GitHub row stores `key` and `username` side by side from its own sign-in. Derived pairs are always *reported*; adopting them takes the flag; explicit wins a disagreement and the conflict is printed; a login the wall recorded under two ids (a reclaimed login) is derived for neither. **Redis write** — one Lua script per message: the same `EXISTS` guard as the reaction script (nothing touches a message that is gone, so a reactions hash never outlives its message), a compare-and-set on the row (written only if it still reads byte-for-byte as it did when the plan was made, so a concurrent DELETE is a no-op rather than a resurrection — the script reports the row as skipped and exits 2 so the operator re-runs), then the field moves — `HGET`/`HEXISTS`/`HSET`/`HDEL` per pair, all inside the one atomic step. The script reads through a client with automatic deserialisation OFF, so the row's exact string is what the compare-and-set sends back and a numeric-looking login field stays a string. `--json` runs the identical plan over the dev file store and lands it by temp file + rename, as `jsonDriver.js` does. **Operator flow** — `node --env-file=.env.local scripts/guestbook-migrate-identity.mjs`: dry run by default, printing every author backfill (with the avatar's id beside an unmapped login and the exact `--map` to adopt it), every field move or merge, every unmapped field, and the derived pairs; `--apply` writes. Credentials come from the environment only (the `KV_REST_API_*` / `UPSTASH_*` names the driver reads) and are never printed. **What it never does** — treat a login as a key: an unmapped row or field is listed and left exactly as it was, and no route grew a login fallback. `identity.js`, `redisDriver.js` and `route.js` now point at the migration where they described legacy rows. **Tests** (`legacyIdentity.test.js`) — the avatar-id parse (numbered subdomains, no id from a login-form or foreign URL), mapping validation (case folding, `github:`-prefixed and numeric ids accepted, bad logins / ids / duplicates refused, merge precedence with conflicts reported), author classification, derivation from keyed rows and legacy avatars with a two-id login derived for neither, the author plan (mapped → owned by the key and not by the login's new holder, unmapped → null with the avatar hint, mapping-vs-avatar conflict flagged, legacy Google → key with the username dropped, keyed/anonymous untouched), reaction folds (move, merge keeping the keyed value, two logins to one account, unmapped left in place, a numeric-looking login still a login), the whole-message plan, and the Lua's shape plus its ARGV builder and reply reader. The script was exercised end to end on a fixture wall in `--json` mode: dry run, `--derive`, `--apply` with an explicit `--map`, an idempotent re-run, and refused input. |

#### `/guestbook` composer — the autosaved draft is scoped to the signed-in account, not the browser

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the composer's draft lived under one `localStorage` key (`guestbook:draft:v1`) for the whole browser. A visitor who left an unsent message and signed out handed it to whoever signed in next on the same browser — the composer restored the first account's private text into the second account's field, under a "Restored your unsent message" banner. Asked to scope the key to the stable session identity, and to leave the old unscoped key unread rather than migrate it to an unknown user. |
| **Files** | **new** `src/lib/guestbook/draftKey.js`, `tests/unit/draftKey.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/guestbook/MessageInput.jsx`, `src/components/guestbook/GuestbookWall.jsx`, `src/hooks/useFieldDraft.js`, `tests/unit/useFieldDraft.test.js`, `tests/e2e/guestbook-composer.spec.js` (reads the draft from the per-account slot), `src/lib/guestbook/identity.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/sessionCallbacks.js`, `src/auth.js` (comments), `README.md` |
| **Details** | **One slot per account.** `draftKeyFor(session.user)` names the slot `guestbook:draft:v2:<identity key>` — the `github:<id>` / `google:<sub>` key `identity.js` mints, the one field that names the account and survives a GitHub rename — and answers null for a session without a key (a JWT minted before keys existed): `useFieldDraft` now takes a null `storageKey` as "persist nothing" — no read, no write, no remove; `clearDraft` still empties the field — so an unkeyed session saves nothing rather than something under the wrong person. **A different person is a different composer.** `GuestbookWall` keys `MessageInput` by the identity, so switching accounts in place remounts it with an empty field and its own slot read once at mount; the hook documents that it assumes a stable key for the life of a mount, and this is what makes that hold — one account's in-progress text can never be autosaved under the next account's slot. **The old slot is never read.** Whatever `guestbook:draft:v1` holds belongs to an unknown earlier account; migrating it would be the same leak in a different order, so the composer removes it on mount and no code path reads it. **The claim corrected.** `identity.js`, `sessionCallbacks.js`, `auth.js`, `GuestbookWall.jsx` and the README said the wall's client code never reads `session.user.key`; it now reads its own key for exactly this one local purpose and never puts it on the wire, and the guarantee is stated as it actually holds. **What scoping does not do:** the slot keeps a signed-out account's draft in the browser for that account's return (the feature), so anyone with DevTools on the same profile can still read `localStorage` — as with any client-side storage; the fix is that the UI never restores one account's text into another's field. **Tests** — `draftKey.test.js`: the slot follows the key (a rename keeps it, another account never shares it), no key → null, the legacy key is a distinct name; `useFieldDraft.test.js` adds a null-key case (nothing read, written or removed; a pre-existing entry untouched through typing, emptying and `clearDraft`) and an A → B → A same-browser sequence (B mounts empty with no banner, A's words come back to A, B's stay B's). |

#### `/guestbook` — a signed-in but keyless legacy session gets the sign-in prompt, not a composer that can never submit

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: an Auth.js session minted before identity keys existed is still `authenticated`, but `session.user.key` is absent and `viewerFromSession()` rejects every write with `401`. The wall gated the composer and the reaction bars on `status === 'authenticated'` alone, so such a session saw a fully enabled composer whose every post failed, and reaction bars whose every tap failed, instead of being told to sign in again. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/guestbookWallWriteGate.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/guestbook/GuestbookWall.jsx`, `src/components/guestbook/SignInPrompt.jsx`, `src/lib/guestbook/identity.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/sessionCallbacks.js`, `src/auth.js` (comments), `README.md` |
| **Details** | **One rule, both sides.** `GuestbookWall` now derives `viewer = viewerFromSession(session)` for an authenticated session — the very function the routes use to decide a write — and `canWrite = viewer !== null` gates the compose slot AND every card's `canReact`; the composer is keyed by `viewer.key` (no `'keyless'` fallback is reachable any more). **The prompt's second voice.** `SignInPrompt` takes `reauth`: the same card and the same GitHub / Google buttons (signing in again runs the provider flow afresh and the `jwt` callback mints the key), headed "Sign in again to leave a message" with one line saying why — the current sign-in predates the wall's account keys, so it can read but not write. The compose slot is now: composer for a write-capable session; nothing while the session resolves; otherwise the prompt, in its re-auth voice exactly when `status` is `authenticated`. **Docs corrected** where `identity.js`, `sessionCallbacks.js`, `auth.js` and the README said keyless writes "answer 401 until the person signs in again": they now say the wall shows that person the prompt rather than a composer, and the client's local uses of `session.user.key` are stated as two (the write gate and the per-account draft slot), never a send. **Tests** — `guestbookWallWriteGate.test.js` renders the real wall with its data hooks and heavy children replaced by probes: resolving → neither composer nor prompt; signed out → the plain prompt, reacting off; a signed-in KEYLESS session → the re-auth prompt, no composer, reacting off; a keyed session → the composer for that identity, reacting on, no prompt. |

#### `/guestbook` — a deep-link fragment is a message id only when it is shaped like one; `#guestbook` no longer crawls the wall

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `messageIdFromHash()` returned any decodable fragment, and the wall hands its answer to `loadUntil()`, which walks older pages until the mark appears — up to ten 50-message fetches. The page defines ordinary anchors of its own (`id="guestbook"` on the title), so visiting `/guestbook#guestbook` started a deep-link crawl for a mark that could not exist. Asked to validate the decoded value against the message-id shape before returning it. |
| **Files** | **new** `src/lib/guestbook/messageId.js`, `tests/unit/messageId.test.js`; **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/deepLink.js`, `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js`, `tests/unit/deepLink.test.js`, `tests/e2e/guestbook-composer.spec.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **Minted and recognised in one place.** `messageId.js` owns the shape `msg_<epoch ms>_<8 hex>`: `mintMessageId()` is what `POST /api/guestbook` now calls (byte-identical output to the inline template it replaces) and `isMessageId()` is what the deep link checks — the two cannot drift, and a test pins that every mint is recognised. **The deep link validates after decoding.** `messageIdFromHash()` still decodes a percent-encoded fragment and still treats an undecodable one as nothing; it now answers `''` for anything the API could not have minted — `#guestbook`, `#top`, `#msg_google`, an id missing its hex tail, upper-case hex, a trailing path — so the wall's deep-link effect never calls `loadUntil()` for a page anchor, and a well-formed-but-absent id remains bounded by the existing ten-fetch cap. **Fixtures.** The e2e composer spec seeded `msg_<n>` ids and deep-linked to `#msg_1`; those ids never matched the minted shape, so the spec now seeds minted-shape ids through one `mid(n)` helper and its locators and deep link go through it too. **Tests** — `messageId.test.js`: the mint's shape, same-millisecond distinctness, and a rejection table (anchors, near misses, wrong tail length and case, whitespace, non-strings); `deepLink.test.js` adds the anchor table and a decode-then-validate pair (an encoded real id comes through, an encoded anchor does not). |

#### `/guestbook` signature pad — a missing 2D context leaves the panel and its preset marks standing

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `HTMLCanvasElement.getContext('2d')` is nullable, but the pad's callback ref dereferenced it immediately (`ctx.setTransform`), as did the live painter, the resize repaint and clear. With canvas rendering unavailable — disabled by policy, a headless or resource-starved renderer, a lost context — opening the optional signature panel threw instead of leaving the preset marks usable. Asked to guard context acquisition and make the fit/draw/redraw/clear paths no-op without a context, as the reaction canvas already does. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/hooks/useSignaturePad.js`, `tests/unit/useSignaturePad.test.js` |
| **Details** | One `contextOf(canvas)` helper answers `getContext('2d') ?? null`, and every painter goes through it: the fit sizes the bitmap and returns before the transform and repaint when there is no context; the live segment and the pointer-up tail are painted only when there is one; `drawSegment` and `redraw` now take the context rather than re-acquiring it; `clear` wipes the bitmap only when it can, and resets the strokes, `hasInk` and the serialiser regardless — so with no context the pad's state machine still works and a clear still clears. **Reproduced first**: a new case attaches a canvas whose `getContext` returns null and drew, lifted, resized, cleared and detached; before the change it failed on attach with `TypeError: Cannot read properties of null (reading 'setTransform')`, after it every step no-ops, `hasInk` toggles as before, and `toPathString` still serialises. |

#### `/guestbook` signature grammar — y is bounded by the viewbox's height, so ink can no longer escape the glyph's box

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review (security): the validator capped every coordinate at 100, but the signature viewbox is 100×40. An authenticated caller could submit y coordinates of 41–100 that passed both gates — the route's store check and the glyph's render check are the same function — and, through the glyph's `overflow-visible` SVG, painted ink outside the intended signature area over the rest of the card. Asked to validate x against `SIGNATURE_VIEWBOX.width` and y against `SIGNATURE_VIEWBOX.height`, with a regression test for y = 41. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/signature.js`, `tests/unit/signature.test.js` |
| **Details** | Every command's arguments are (x, y) pairs in order — M/L one pair, Q control + end, C two controls + end — so the tokenizer now bounds the even-indexed argument of each command by the viewbox width and the odd-indexed one by its height, control points included (a control point can pull a curve as far out as an end point can). The pad's serialiser already clamped each axis to its own bound and every preset mark sits inside 40, so nothing legitimate changes. **Reproduced first** against the shipped module: `M 10 10 L 90 41`, `L 90 100`, a `Q` control at y 41 and a `C` control at y 100 were all accepted while x 101 was refused. **Tests** — the far corner `L 100 40` passes; one unit past it on either axis fails, as does `40.01`; y = 41 or 100 is refused in every slot it can occupy (M, L, Q control and end, C's two controls and end); an x of 41–100 in those same slots still passes. Any stored path that already carried a y above 40 now renders nothing rather than something, which is the render gate's documented contract. |

#### Unit-test runner — the Oxc transform no longer reaches into `node_modules`

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the Vitest Oxc filter's first `include` pattern matches dependency paths such as `/node_modules/pkg/index.jsx` (and the `src/*.js` pattern matches any package shipping a `src/` directory), while `exclude: []` removed nothing — so the config's own claim that node_modules never match was false, and with the language pinned to `jsx` an inlined dependency's `.ts` or `.jsx` file would be re-parsed and could fail. Asked to exclude `node_modules` or restrict the include to project source. |
| **Files** | **changed** `vitest.config.mjs` |
| **Details** | `exclude: [/\/node_modules\//]` replaces the empty list; the include stays as it was so `src/**/*.js` JSX still transforms, and the comment now states the real filter behaviour — confirmed by running the include regexes against sample paths: `src/app/page.js` matches, `node_modules/pkg/index.jsx`, `node_modules/pkg/src/index.js` and `node_modules/pkg/dist/index.mts` all matched before the exclusion. Vitest externalises node_modules by default, so this is a guard for inlined dependencies rather than a hot path. The full unit suite, including the motion-toggle suite that imports a JSX-in-`.js` page, passes unchanged. |

#### `/journey` atlas — a dimmed bar is de-emphasised, not disabled: mouse and touch can jump from it as the keyboard already could

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review (accessibility): dimmed atlas bars remained focusable buttons that invoked `jump()` from the keyboard, but their dimmed class carried `pointer-events-none`, so mouse and touch users could not invoke the same control — one button, two behaviours by input method. The track chips are described as highlighting a track rather than disabling one (and `TimelineNode`'s `dimmed` only fades the card), so the ask was to keep dimmed bars pointer-interactive. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/timelineAtlasDimmed.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/journey/TimelineAtlas.jsx` |
| **Details** | The dimmed class drops `pointer-events-none` and keeps `opacity-[0.13]`; it also gains `hover:opacity-70` and `focus-visible:opacity-100`, so a pointer can read the bar it is about to jump to and keyboard focus on a dimmed bar is visible rather than a ring at 13% — a small addition beyond the letter of the finding, in the same spirit (the `.ja-bar` hover/focus colour states already existed and now simply apply to dimmed bars too). The header comment states the rule: dimmed means de-emphasised, never disabled, for every input. **Tests** — a jsdom suite renders the atlas with the Career track highlighted, with `IntersectionObserver` stubbed for framer's `useInView` and `scrollIntoView` recorded: the Education bar carries the dim class and no pointer cut-off, is not disabled, takes focus, and a click scrolls its `jn-<id>` card into view with the reduced-motion `auto` contract and hands it focus, exactly as a lit bar does; with no track highlighted nothing is dimmed. |

#### `/guestbook` UI sounds — a refused `AudioContext.resume()` no longer escapes as an unhandled rejection

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `AudioContext.resume()` is asynchronous, so its rejection (autoplay policy, `NotAllowedError`) sailed past the synchronous `try/catch` in `useUiSound` and surfaced as an unhandled promise rejection — the opposite of the "audio is decoration, a failed context must never surface" contract the catch block states. Asked to handle the returned promise before scheduling playback. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/useUiSound.test.js`; **changed** `src/hooks/useUiSound.js` |
| **Details** | The resume call becomes `Promise.resolve(sharedCtx.resume()).catch(() => {})` — the rejection is swallowed on the promise itself, and the wrapper means an engine handing back a non-promise cannot throw either; the footer's `pluckSynth` already did the same. Not awaited: Web Audio starts queued nodes the moment the context runs, so the cue is scheduled immediately and plays when resume goes through, or never, silently. **Reproduced first**: a jsdom suite fakes a suspended context whose `resume()` returns a rejected promise and listens on `process` for `unhandledRejection` — before the change the listener received the `NotAllowedError`; after it, nothing, with resume called once and both partials of the `send` cue still scheduled. (The fake's `resume` is a plain function on purpose: a `vi.fn` attaches its own handlers to any promise it returns to record settled results, which would mark the rejection handled and hide the bug — the first cut of the test passed against the broken code for exactly that reason.) |

#### `/guestbook` paging (redis) — a delete racing the page read can no longer end the wall early

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `listMessages` read the index (ZRANGE) and then the rows (MGET) as two steps, and a delete landing between them left nulls that `rowsFor` dropped. If every one of the page's `limit` rows was deleted in that window, `messages` was empty and the read answered `next: null` — even though the extra id it had already fetched, and older live messages, still existed — so the client treated the wall as exhausted and could never page further. Asked to continue scanning from the last indexed position until a live row fills the page or the index is genuinely exhausted. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/redisDriver.js`, `tests/unit/redisDriver.test.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | The read is now a loop over the INDEX: each round walks `need + 1` positions (`need` = what the page still lacks; the extra one is the "more exists" signal), fetches the rows, and — while the page is short and the walk says more exists — scans on from the last **indexed** position rather than the last live row, since there may be none. The index walk returns positions, not ids: `WITHSCORES` gives the ZSET score, which IS a message's `t` (`addMessage` scores by `createdAt`), so a position can be minted for an id whose row is already gone. Continuation stays score-bounded (the same tie + older two-query shape as before), never rank-offset: a rank shifts the moment a member ahead of it is deleted, and a rank continuation would skip the live member that slid into the vacated rank. Each round advances strictly, so the loop ends; the extra rounds cost only what the race deleted. Two invariants fall out and the client already leans on both: a short page means the index is exhausted (`next` null), and a full page's `next` is its last row's position. **Tests** — the driver suite gains a fake ZSET + row store answering `zrange` (rank and score-bounded, `REV`, `WITHSCORES` flat) and `mget`, with index and rows kept separate so "in the index, row gone" is expressible: the regression case (both rows of a two-row page deleted after the index read) now returns the next two live messages with a real cursor and continues by score from the deleted id's own position; plus first/cursor/last-page walks, a same-millisecond tie at a page edge, a partly deleted page filled from further down, a short page only when the index is exhausted, and an empty index. |

#### `/guestbook` no-links filter — public-suffix-aware, so a bare domain on any real TLD is refused

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review (security): the validator's bare-domain check matched a shortlist of fifteen TLDs, so `spam.ai`, `spam.tech`, `spam.zip`, `spam.co.uk` or an IDN passed both gates and landed on the public wall — a straightforward bypass of the stated no-links policy. Asked for a maintained, public-suffix-aware domain detector (or explicit validation of every URL-like hostname) with false-positive handling covered by tests. |
| **Files** | **new** `src/lib/guestbook/limits.js`; **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/validate.js`, `src/components/guestbook/SignInPrompt.jsx`, `src/components/guestbook/MessageInput.jsx`, `src/lib/refineModes.js`, `tests/unit/validate.test.js`, `package.json`, `package-lock.json`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **Detector.** `containsUrl()` refuses a scheme or `www.` as before, and otherwise tokenises the message into dotted runs of hostname labels (Unicode-aware, so punctuation around a token is not part of it) and asks `tldts` — the maintained Public Suffix List, now a direct dependency at the version jsdom already pulled in transitively — whether each is a registrable domain under an ICANN-delegated suffix, or a raw IP. Prose survives on its own merits: `node.js`, `e.g.`, `Ph.D.`, `U.S.`, `9.30`, `1.2.3`, `Mr.Smith` have no real suffix and are not domains. **Two exemptions, both pinned:** dev file names whose extension is also a ccTLD (`README.md`, `main.py`, `run.sh`, `lib.rs`) as bare two-label names only — `www.spam.md`, `https://spam.py` and `docs.spam.sh` still fall — and the site's own `ma.codes`, with `ma.codes.evil.ai` still refused. **Bundle.** Client components only ever needed `MESSAGE_MAX`, and importing it from `validate.js` would have dragged the suffix list into the browser bundle; the length band moved to `limits.js`, which the sign-in prompt, the compose card and the refine modes import, while `validate.js` re-exports it for its server-side callers and a test pins the two in step. **Tests** — twelve bypass shapes refused (any-TLD, subdomain, path, port, caps, wrapping punctuation, trailing full stop, IDN, IP), the explicit shapes still refused, eleven prose sentences kept, the file-name and own-domain exemptions with their limits, and the detector exported for direct assertion. |

#### `/guestbook` writes — the total is settled from the write's own `count`, not from whether a fetched page held the card

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the submit race test only refreshed the newest page, where a confirmed message appears in both the list and `count`. An in-flight OLDER-page fetch (a rail jump, a deep link) can instead return a count that already includes the post without returning its id; the settlement then saw no `data.id` in the list, judged "not polled", and incremented a total that already had it — one too many until the next poll. Asked to add that case and settle the count independently of whether the fetched page contains the message. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js`, `src/hooks/useGuestbookMessages.js`, `tests/unit/useGuestbookMessagesRace.test.js`, `tests/unit/guestbookRoute.test.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **The write answers with the size after it.** `POST` 201 now carries `count` beside the public message — `countMessages()` read just after the store, an O(1) `ZCARD` on Redis — and `DELETE` 200 is `{ ok, count }` likewise. The client can never tell from an older page whether the count it carried included the write (the page cannot list a message newer than its cursor), but the server can say what the wall held the moment the write landed, and that is the only observation that is correct in every interleaving: no fetch in flight, a newest page that listed the message, or an older page that only counted it. **The hook settles from it.** Submit strips `count` off the 201 before the card takes the pending slot (so the field never rides into state) and sets the total to it; the list check that used to decide the total now decides only the card — the polled copy is still dropped before the pending card becomes the real one. Delete mirrors it: the revived card, if a poll brought it back, still goes on the 200, and the total is the 200's count whether or not the fetched page held the card. A response without a count — an older server during a deploy — falls back to the previous list heuristic in both flows, so the pair never regresses. One ordering remains as it was and self-heals on the next poll: a GET served before the write but processed after its settlement overwrites the total with its older count, as any fetch's count always has. **Tests** — the race suite's stand-in wall grows a second leaf behind a cursor and a `count` the test can move ahead of both pages; the new POST case loads the newest page, submits, moves the server's count, pulls the older page via `ensureLoaded` (the count comes back one higher, the list gains only the two old marks) and asserts the 201 settles the total at exactly that — against the pre-fix hook it reads one too many; the DELETE mirror does the same with a count that still includes the removed card, and its complement — the older page served AFTER the delete, count already one down, card absent either way — asserts the 200 keeps that total rather than decrementing it again, which is what "reconciled from the count, not from revival" means in both directions. The reload cases now resolve with `count` and assert it never becomes a card field; the no-count fallback is pinned for both flows. The route suite asserts the Google post's 201 carries `TOTAL + 1` and a new case posts, deletes, and reads `{ ok, count }` with the size after each. |
