#### `/guestbook` timestamps — the absolute-date fallback is UTC, so a card renders one date everywhere

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `timeAgo`'s `29d`→absolute-date fallback formatted in the runtime's local timezone. `MessageCard` is a Client Component that also server-renders, so a timestamp near midnight could format to one date on the UTC server and another in the visitor's zone — a hydration mismatch, and a posting date that changed with where it was read. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/timeAgo.js`, `tests/unit/timeAgo.test.js` |
| **Details** | **One zone, one date.** The fallback's `toLocaleDateString` call now passes `timeZone: 'UTC'`, so the same instant formats to the same `14 Jul 2026` on the server, on every visitor's device and in every test runner, whatever their clock is set to; the relative labels (`just now` … `29d ago`) are zone-free arithmetic on epoch milliseconds and were never affected. Honest scope: the wall's list starts `null` and is fetched client-side, so no card is server-rendered with real data today and the mismatch was not yet reachable — the fix makes the label deterministic now and keeps it so if the wall ever ships initial data from the server. The trade-off is stated plainly: a mark posted at 09:00 in UTC+10 on the 2nd reads as the 1st once it is a month old, for everyone, rather than one date for its author and another for a reader elsewhere. **Tests** — a new case renders 23:30 UTC on 14 Jul with the process timezone flipped to UTC+14 (Kiritimati, where it is already the 15th), UTC−11 (Pago Pago) and UTC, and expects `14 Jul 2026` from all three — with a control asserting the flip really moved the runtime's clock (`getDate()` answers 15 and 14 respectively), so the test cannot pass by the flip silently not applying; the original timezone is restored in a `finally`. |

#### `/guestbook` cursors — the contract now says what the code does: shape-validated, not authenticated

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: base64url encoding is not proof that the server minted a cursor — any caller can encode a valid `timestamp:id` pair and `decodeCursor` accepts it — which contradicted the API contract ("a cursor the server did not mint is a `400`") and the tests named for it. Asked to either authenticate cursors (an HMAC) or revise the contract and tests to state that only shape is validated. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/cursor.js` (comments), `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js` (comment), `tests/unit/paging.test.js`, `tests/unit/guestbookRoute.test.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **The contract is revised, not the code — deliberately.** A cursor is a position into a PUBLIC, newest-first wall, and a position is not a capability: a hand-built one reaches nothing that following `nextCursor` would not, at the same bounded cost, because both drivers consume it as a plain comparison bound — the Redis driver uses `t` as a numeric score bound and compares `id` in JS, never inside a command, and `ID_RE` admits no range syntax, so no crafted id can change what the index is asked. Signing would buy back no privilege and would cost a secret on a public read path, cursors bound to a key (every deploy or rotation turning in-flight walks into `400`s the client hook treats as failures), and one more variable for the hermetic e2e server. So the header of `cursor.js`, its decoder's comment, the route's header and the README's paging note now say exactly that: a *malformed* cursor is a `400`; a well-formed one is served, whoever built it; cursors are opaque by convention, exact by construction, public by design. (This supersedes the "did not mint" wording in the paging entry below.) **Tests say it too.** The codec's rejection case is renamed for what it checks — anything that is not cursor-shaped — and a new case pins the acceptance side: a hand-built, well-formed cursor decodes to its position, minting that position yields the very same bytes (there is no secret in a cursor), and a position no message occupies is still a position. The route suite's `400` case is renamed "refuses a malformed cursor", and a new case proves the contract end to end over the json driver: the cursor built by hand from the seed's own timestamp for `msg_4` is byte-for-byte the `nextCursor` the server mints after a four-message page, paging from it serves `msg_3, msg_2, msg_1`, and a cursor for a position between two messages pages from where it would sit. |

#### `/guestbook` pagination — the page rail is constant-size in the wall: graduations are a CSS tile, not a span per page

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `WallPagination` rendered one decorative tick `<span>` per page, so the control's DOM grew linearly with the wall even though only eight cards are mounted — on a long-lived wall, hundreds or thousands of ticks re-rendered on every page or count change. Asked for a bounded set of major ticks or a CSS repeating gradient so the control stays constant-size. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/wallPaginationTicks.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/guestbook/WallPagination.jsx`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **Both halves of the ask.** The graduations are now a 1px `linear-gradient` tick tiled across the rail by `background-size` / `repeat-x`, so the rail is the same five nodes (hairline, filament, dim scale, lit scale, comet) at two pages or two thousand; a flip or a count change updates two style strings and nothing in the tree. The tile is `(100% − 1px) / tiles` wide so the last tick lands inside the box at the far end instead of a pixel past it (the old last span sat at `left: 100%`, overflowing). The lit-before-current distinction survives as a second layer of the same tiles clipped with `clip-path: inset()` to the filament's reach plus the tick's own pixel — so page one's tick is lit at 0%, and at 100% the inset is floored at zero with `max()` rather than going negative. **Bounded, too:** past `MAX_TICK_INTERVALS` (28) page gaps the scale thins to every k-th page — `tickTiles()` picks the smallest even step that fits — because on the 280px rail that is a 10px minimum pitch, below which graduations read as a smear rather than a scale; the rail's job is the comet's position on the whole, which the filament and the odometer carry exactly. Prev/next, the aria-live line, the click-to-jump hit-test and the odometer are untouched. **Tests** — a jsdom component test pins that 2, 19, 500 and 5,000 pages render an identical tree of at most eight rail nodes; that flipping to page 18 and growing the count to 1,200 change nothing in the tree; the tiling arithmetic (one tile per gap up to the cap, an even thinning past it, never more than the cap nor fewer than half of it, no divide-by-zero at one page); that both layers carry the repeated gradient sized from the tile count; the lit clip at both ends; and the controls that matter — disabled states at the ends, "Page N of M", next → `onPage(1)`, a click at the rail's midpoint → `onPage(9)` of 19, and no control at all for a single page. |

#### `/guestbook` signature — a keyboard can sign: the preset marks return as real buttons beneath the pad

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the signature feature was pointer-only. A canvas is not focusable and has no keyboard model, so a keyboard-only visitor on a device that still reports a pointer (a laptop, a tablet with a keyboard) could not add a signature at all; the canvas's `aria-label` named the control without making it operable. The review asked for a keyboard-accessible alternative such as the preset marks `signature.js` already defines. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/signatureFieldMarks.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/guestbook/SignatureField.jsx`, `src/lib/guestbook/signature.js` (comment), `README.md` |
| **Details** | **Drawing stays the primary path; the marks are the keyboard's.** The four `PRESET_MARKS` — which never left the serialiser and are still asserted valid against the grammar in the unit suite — render beneath the pad as a labelled group ("Or pick a mark") of native `<button>`s carrying `aria-pressed`, the reaction bar's own pattern: in the Tab order, activated by Space and Enter, announced as "Flourish, toggle button, pressed", with no roving focus or key handling to get wrong. Each button draws its own path in the signature's 100×40 space, so what is picked is exactly the glyph the card will carry. **The two inputs are exclusive by construction**: choosing a mark clears the pad and hands the parent that mark's path; a stroke that serialises drops the mark and hands over the drawn path; a gesture that never became a stroke leaves a chosen mark alone rather than wiping it with a null. Clear, Discard and the post-send reset all drop a chosen mark exactly as they drop ink, and the pad's canvas keeps its label, now pointing at the marks. **No pointer at all** (`(any-pointer: none)`) used to render nothing; the section now renders, and its open panel is the marks alone — no pad nobody could draw on, no drawing hint — under a "Pick a mark" label. (An earlier pass had removed the marks on owner direction; they return here on the review's finding, in the field's existing chrome.) **Tests** — a jsdom component test drives the real field with the pad's recording context, a fixed css box and the pointer-capture stubs: every preset is a `BUTTON` at `tabIndex` 0, enabled, un-pressed, in a group named "Or pick a mark", and pressing one emits its path (valid against the grammar) and marks it pressed; a second mark replaces the first, and pressing the chosen one again emits null; Clear drops a chosen mark; a stroke through real pointer events after choosing a mark emits the drawn path and un-presses the mark; choosing a mark after drawing issues a `clearRect`, emits the preset and returns the pad's border to dashed; with `(any-pointer: none)` matching there is no canvas and no hint, only a "Pick a mark" group that signs; Discard emits null and folds the marks away, and a bumped `resetSignal` folds the panel without a further emit, reopening clean. |

#### `/guestbook` reactions — a canvas with no 2D context no longer swallows the click

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `getContext('2d')` is nullable, and `ReactionBar`'s ember burst dereferenced it unguarded — `ctx.scale` threw before the reaction request was started. The burst runs synchronously inside the click handler ahead of `onReact`, so a browser with canvas rendering unavailable (disabled by policy, a headless or resource-starved renderer, a lost context) lost the reaction itself to a decoration. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/reactionBarBurst.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/guestbook/ReactionBar.jsx` |
| **Details** | **Decoration no-ops; it never throws.** `burst` now asks for the context first and, when the browser hands back `null`, returns a shared no-op cancel before touching the canvas at all — no resize, no `scale`, no frame scheduled — so `handleClick` proceeds straight to the sound cue and the POST. Ordering is otherwise unchanged: the context is obtained before the canvas is resized, and since resizing resets a context's state without invalidating the object, the `scale` that follows draws exactly as before. The cancel contract holds too — the unmount cleanup's `cancelRef.current?.()` still has a function to call, same as the sound hook, which already treats a failed `AudioContext` as nothing to surface. **Tests** — a jsdom component test drives the real bar through a click with `HTMLCanvasElement.prototype.getContext` stubbed to `null` and reduced motion forced off: the burst was attempted (the guard, not the motion preference, is what stopped it), `onReact` still received the message id, key and `clearing: false`, and the pending lock released; a second click on another reaction goes out the same way; and with a working context stubbed in, `scale` runs once and one frame is scheduled — the guard is not a kill switch. |

#### Message refine — a send in progress aborts the refine instead of hiding it

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: submitting the guestbook composer while a refinement was streaming only set `disabled`; `MessageRefine` hid its panel but never aborted or reset `useMessageRefine`. Because that composer stays mounted after a successful post, the old stream could finish and the stale suggestion reappear beneath the cleared input. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/messageRefineAbort.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/contact/MessageRefine.jsx`, `src/components/guestbook/MessageInput.jsx` (comment) |
| **Details** | **Reset at the transition.** `MessageRefine` now runs `reset()` — which aborts the in-flight controller and clears status, suggestion and error — whenever `disabled` flips on, so the moment a form starts sending, any refine in flight or on offer is gone rather than parked. The hook's existing ownership guard does the rest: a response whose body was already buffered still resolves after the abort, and the read loop cancels its reader and exits without committing, so nothing can surface later. Idempotent on an idle hook (mounting already-disabled is a no-op); after a failed send the field is restored and a fresh refine is one tap away. The contact form remounts after a successful send and was never exposed, but shares the fix. **Tests** — a jsdom component test drives the real hook through a hand-fed `ReadableStream` behind a stubbed `fetch`: mid-stream, flipping `disabled` on removes the panel and aborts the request's signal; the stream then completes anyway and `disabled` clears, and no panel, no "Use this" and none of the rewrite's text return — only the affordance; a finished suggestion on offer is discarded the same way; mounting already disabled fetches and shows nothing. |

#### `/guestbook` API — a Google author's account id no longer leaves the server; ownership travels as `isOwn`

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: Google identities are documented as internal-only, but the GET's `...msg` spread sent `author.username` (`google:<sub>`) for every stored Google-authored message through a public endpoint. Hiding it only in `MessageCard` does not stop a client reading the stable Google account identifier off the network response. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js`, `src/hooks/useGuestbookMessages.js`, `src/components/guestbook/GuestbookWall.jsx`, `tests/unit/guestbookRoute.test.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **One public shape, on the wire.** The route gains `toPublicMessage`, used by GET and the POST 201 alike: it strips the private reactions map as before, replaces the author with `publicAuthor` — `username` ships only for GitHub authors (messages stored before the provider field existed are all GitHub; and since `':'` is illegal in a GitHub login, a username carrying one is treated as internal whatever the provider says) — and adds `isOwn`, decided server-side from the session against the STORED author with the same case-insensitive comparison DELETE makes. The stored record is untouched: the id is still what DELETE's ownership check compares against. **The client compares nothing.** `GuestbookWall`'s bin-button gate is now `msg.isOwn || viewerIsAdmin` instead of a username comparison, the DELETE toast copy reads the same flag, and the optimistic card mirrors the public shape — a GitHub login carried, a Google username not, `isOwn: true` by construction — so the pending card, the 201 swap and a polled copy all agree. `MessageCard` already fell back to the display name when no username was present, so it needed no change. **Tests** — the route suite seeds a Google-authored mark beside the GitHub ones and asserts on the raw response text: no `google:` anywhere in a public GET, the Google author reduced to name/avatar/provider, the GitHub author's handle intact, every `isOwn` false for an anonymous viewer; `isOwn` true for exactly the Google viewer's own mark (still no id on the wire) and, case-insensitively, for a GitHub viewer's; a POST from a Google session answers 201 with `isOwn: true` and no id, while the stored record keeps it. |

#### `/guestbook` reactions — a per-user budget on the one authenticated mutation that had none

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: every authenticated call to `/api/guestbook/reactions` performs a Redis script/write, but the endpoint had no rate limit — a single valid session could generate unbounded Redis and server traffic by toggling a reaction in a loop, unlike message posting and presence heartbeats, which are explicitly metered. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/reactionsRoute.test.js`; **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/ratelimit.js`, `src/app/api/guestbook/reactions/route.js`, `tests/unit/ratelimit.test.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **`checkReactionRateLimit(username)`** joins the two existing limits in `ratelimit.js`: `REACTIONS_PER_MINUTE` (30) per session username in a sliding window — generous for a person clicking through the wall, a hard ceiling for a script. With Redis it is the same `@upstash/ratelimit` sliding window the others use, under `guestbook:rl:reactions:` with the in-process denied-key cache; it follows the POSTING rule for backend choice (an explicit json driver keeps it in memory even when Redis credentials are present, so the e2e's hermetic server never writes limiter keys upstream), not the presence one. The in-memory fallback is now ONE helper (`memoryWindow`) behind both presence and reactions, so the two windows cannot drift in semantics — presence's behaviour is unchanged and its suite still passes as written. The route checks the budget **after** auth and body validation (an anonymous or malformed request spends nothing) and **before** `setReaction` (a refused call costs the store nothing), answering `429` with `Retry-After` and a message the client's existing error toast shows verbatim. **Tests** — the limiter suite gains the reaction window's three cases (budget then refusal with an honest retry time; per-user keying; the slide freeing exactly one slot). A new route suite drives the real handler over the json driver with the session stubbed: a full budget of toggles is served with each `viewerReaction` reflected, the next call is a `429` with `Retry-After` and the stored reaction is still the last accepted one; another user is served; a budget's worth of malformed bodies are all `400`s that leave the first valid reaction within budget; an anonymous caller is a `401` before anything is metered. |

That backend is ephemeral on Vercel, so the API accepted posts that disappeared on a cold start — and a console warning does not make that safe. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/store.js`, `tests/unit/store.test.js`, `README.md`, `.env.example` |

The explicit path is preserved: `GUESTBOOK_DRIVER=json` under production still serves, with the existing warning, which is how the e2e suite boots its hermetic production server with the Redis variables emptied. The store's header and the README's storage note describe the new rule. **Tests** — the facade suite's final block now covers five load outcomes with the module registry reset between them: forced redis without credentials refuses (as before); a served production with no Redis and no driver refuses with an error naming the variables and `GUESTBOOK_DRIVER=json`; explicit json under production serves and warns; the build phase loads with neither throw nor warning; development auto-selects json without a warning. |

#### `/guestbook` json driver — a data file it cannot read is an error, never an empty wall to overwrite

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: `readAll`'s catch treated every read failure — permission and I/O errors, malformed JSON — as an empty wall. Every mutation is read-modify-write, so the next successful post or reaction then overwrote the existing file with only the new record, turning a transient error or corruption into silent data loss. |
| **Files** | **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/jsonDriver.js`, `tests/unit/store.test.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **Only `ENOENT` is an empty store.** `readAll` now catches the file read alone and returns `[]` solely for a missing file — the first run, before anything was written. Any other I/O error (`EACCES`, `EISDIR`, a disk fault) is rethrown as it is, with its code; content that does not parse throws a descriptive error naming the path (with the parse error as `cause`), and valid JSON that is not an array of messages throws its own; an empty or whitespace-only file is the one lenient case, since it holds nothing a write could lose. Because the read runs before the temp-file write begins, a failing mutation rejects with the file on disk untouched and no temp debris, the route answers 500, and the serialised queue carries on. The driver's header now lists this as its third invariant, and the README's storage note says the same. **Tests** — five cases in the store suite, each checking the disk afterwards: a missing file is empty across `getMessages` / `countMessages` / `listMessages`; an empty file is empty and the first write lands; malformed JSON fails every read and every mutation (`add`, `setReaction`, `delete`) with the file byte-for-byte as it was and no temp file started; a JSON object instead of an array is refused the same way; a path that is a directory rejects with `EISDIR` from reads and writes alike, and the queue still accepts the next mutation on a good path. |

Raised together, as the finding asked, rather than pinning an older test stack: `engines.node` is now `^22.13.0 \|\| ^24.0.0` in `package.json` and the lockfile's mirrored root entry; the launcher's `ENGINE_FLOOR` in `scripts/supported-node.mjs` — the run-time half of the guard, which re-execs Next under a supported nvm build and must agree with the install-time half — is `[22, 13, 0]`, its comment now says where the floor comes from, and its "no usable build" message names the new range; the README badge (≥ 22.13), the Requirements line, and CONTRIBUTING's table say the same and name the test stack as the reason. #### `/guestbook` Redis driver — reaction check-and-write and message delete are atomic

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the reaction path's existence check and its mutation were separate Redis operations. A concurrent delete could remove the message after `EXISTS` succeeded, after which `HSET` recreated an orphan reactions hash — the exact invariant the guard claimed to enforce. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/redisDriver.test.js`; **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/redisDriver.js` |
| **Details** | **One script, one step.** `setReaction` now runs a Lua script (`SET_REACTION_LUA`, via the client's `createScript` — `EVALSHA` first, `EVAL` on a `NOSCRIPT` miss): `EXISTS` on the message key, then `HSET` or `HDEL` on the reactions hash, then `HGETALL`, all inside one atomic server-side execution and one round trip instead of three. A missing message replies nil and the driver returns `null`, as before. The script runs on a second client over the same credentials with `automaticDeserialization` **off**: the reply is `HGETALL`'s flat field/value array, and the default client JSON-parses every array element it can, so a numeric-looking login (`"123"`, `"1e5"`) would have become a number before it became an object key; the driver builds the map itself with string keys. **The other side too.** `deleteMessage` is now one `MULTI`/`EXEC` of `ZREM` + `DEL`(message, reactions) rather than two commands, so no reader — the script's `EXISTS` included — can see a message half-deleted; a reaction therefore either lands before the whole delete (and the `DEL` takes it with the message) or finds no message at all. `addMessage`'s `SET` + `ZADD` moved from a plain pipeline to a transaction for the same reason: a page walk never meets an indexed id whose row is not yet written. **Tests** — a new suite mocks `@upstash/redis` with a recording stand-in (no Redis is contacted; the placeholder credentials are exactly that) and pins the command shapes: `setReaction` is one script call with both keys, the username and the key (`''` to clear), with no client-side `EXISTS`/`HSET`/`HGETALL`; the flat reply becomes a map with string keys, `"123"` included; a nil reply is `null`; the script's source checks, writes and reads back on the server; the script client has deserialisation off while the data client keeps it on; `deleteMessage` is one transaction of `ZREM` + `DEL` reporting the `ZREM` count; `addMessage` is one transaction of `SET` + `ZADD`. The script itself was not executed against a live Redis in this change. |

#### `/guestbook` signature pad — the ink on screen now ends where the posted path ends

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: pointer moves painted only midpoint-to-midpoint segments and pointer-up never painted the final midpoint-to-last-sample segment, while `strokesToPath` does append that tail — so the signature displayed while drawing was shorter than the one that got posted, and the repaint after a resize omitted the tail too. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/useSignaturePad.test.js`; **changed** `src/lib/guestbook/signature.js`, `src/hooks/useSignaturePad.js`, `tests/unit/signature.test.js` |
| **Details** | **One geometry, three consumers.** `signature.js` gains `strokeSegments(stroke)`: the single description of a stroke's centreline as `Q` (through a sample to the next midpoint) and `L` (the straight tail from the last midpoint to the last sample; a lone sample is a zero-length dot) segments, each carrying its own start point. `strokesToPath` now formats that list — its output is byte-for-byte what it was, which the existing budget and smoothing tests confirm — and the pad's two painters consume the same list instead of their own copies of the construction: the live painter paints the one segment each new sample completes (the straight provisional lead on the second sample, then each curve as the third sample onward closes it), pointer-up — and pointer-cancel, which shares the handler — paints the tail once a curve exists (a two-sample stroke's lead already is its tail; a down-up dot stays a single sample), and the resize repaint traces the whole list, tail included. Midpoint smoothing always leaves the ink half a sample short of the pen until the stroke ends; that gap is exactly the tail, and now every renderer agrees on it. **Tests** — `strokeSegments` is pinned in the signature suite (continuity: each segment starts where the previous ended; the tail lands on the last sample; two samples give one straight segment; one sample gives a dot; and `strokesToPath` is exactly the segments formatted). A new hook suite under jsdom installs a recording 2D context and replays a five-sample stroke: while the pen is down the last op is the curve to the last midpoint and no line reaches the final sample; pointer-up records precisely `moveTo(last midpoint) → lineTo(last sample)` and the serialised path ends on that point; a two-sample stroke and a dot get no phantom tail; and a doubled box's repaint traces exactly the serialiser's segments over the rescaled samples while the serialised signature stays unchanged. |

#### ⌘K command palette — the active option now follows the keyboard into view

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review: the shared palette keeps focus on its input and drives the listbox with `aria-activedescendant` — and changing that attribute scrolls nothing. The Journey palette has enough actions to overflow the list's 19rem cap, so a keyboard user could arrow onto an option below the fold and Enter would still run it, unseen. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/commandPaletteScroll.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/commandPalette/CommandPalette.jsx` |
| **Details** | An effect in the component scrolls the active option's row — its `<li>` wrapper, so a section header directly above it comes into view too — to the nearest edge (`scrollIntoView({ block: 'nearest' })`) whenever the selection changes: a no-op while the row is already visible, a one-row nudge at either edge, a jump to the top on wrap-around. Keyed on the active option's id *and* the filtered list, so a filter change that lands the selection on a different row follows it as well. Instant rather than smooth: native listboxes do not animate selection, and a smooth scroll would lag rapid arrowing. Pointer selection (`onMouseMove`) is excluded through a consumed ref flag — the cursor is already on a hovered row, and nudging the list under a moving cursor would re-select whatever slid beneath it. The hook is untouched; the fix is rendering-side only. **Tests** — the first component test in the suite (`@testing-library/react` under jsdom, `scrollIntoView` stubbed since jsdom has no layout): each arrow-down scrolls the newly-selected row with `block: 'nearest'`, twenty-one presses deep; arrow-up from the top wraps to the last row and scrolls to it; a filter that moves the selection follows it; a hover selects without scrolling and the next arrow key scrolls again. |

#### `/guestbook` — the wall is read a page at a time: cursor pagination on `GET /api/guestbook`, a separately-counted total, a poll that fetches only the newest page, and one live-region announcement per batch of arrivals

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review of the `/guestbook` branch: every public `GET` — and every client's 30-second poll — loaded, sorted and returned the entire unbounded message set, and fetched every reactions hash with it. The per-user posting limit caps one person's rate, not the number of people or the wall's lifetime growth, so payload size, Redis commands and polling cost all grew without bound. A second finding on the same poll path: when one poll discovered several messages, the wall's aria-live effect called `setAnnouncement` once per message, React batched the writes, and assistive technology heard only the final arrival despite the stated per-message behaviour. A third: a poll can observe the newly stored server message before the POST response arrives; the load then kept the optimistic card and added the real id, and the 201's swap turned the optimistic card into that same id — two duplicate cards. |
| **Files** | **new** `src/lib/guestbook/paging.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/cursor.js`, `tests/unit/paging.test.js`, `tests/unit/guestbookRoute.test.js`, `tests/unit/events.test.js`, `tests/unit/useGuestbookMessagesRace.test.js`; **changed** `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/store.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/redisDriver.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/jsonDriver.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/events.js`, `src/hooks/useGuestbookMessages.js`, `src/components/guestbook/GuestbookWall.jsx`, `tests/unit/store.test.js`, `tests/e2e/guestbook-composer.spec.js`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **The read contract.** `GET /api/guestbook` now serves ONE page — `?limit=` (default 8, hard-capped at 50) newest-first messages from `?cursor=` — plus the wall's total `count`, read on its own (`ZCARD` on Redis; the file length on the dev driver) so the size is known without loading the wall, and an opaque `nextCursor` (`null` on the last page). Reactions are fetched for the page's ids only, so a `GET` costs O(limit) commands however long the wall grows. A cursor is the *position* of a page's last message — `(createdAt ms, id)`, base64url — not an offset, so it stays exact when new marks land at the top or old ones are deleted; `decodeCursor` validates every field and the route answers a cursor it did not mint with a `400`. **One order, two drivers.** "Newest first" is `createdAt` descending with ties broken by id descending — the order a Redis ZSET walks in `REV` mode — pinned in `paging.js` and used by both drivers, so a same-millisecond pair of posts is never skipped at a page edge: the Redis driver's cursor page is two index reads in one pipeline (the same-score members below the cursor's id, then everything with a lower score) followed by one bounded `MGET`; the json driver sorts by the identical comparator. `getMessages()` (the full scan) survives only for the dev rate limiter's last-post walk and the contract suite, and the store's contract now says so; `DELETE`'s ownership check reads one key (`getMessage`) instead of the wall. **The client holds a prefix, not the wall.** `useGuestbookMessages` keeps a newest-first prefix: the first fetch is two leaves (current + one ahead); a page flip calls `ensureLoaded`, which appends bounded pages from the cursor until the target leaf *and the one after it* are in hand (so *next* is always instant, and a rail jump far down the wall is a few requests, never one unbounded one); the poll re-reads only the newest page and *merges* it (`mergeNewestPage`: arrivals glow, the window the page covers is replaced by the server's copy — a card deleted elsewhere leaves, reaction counts refresh — pending optimistic cards and a just-confirmed own post survive on top, and the older local tail is kept, or dropped when a short page proves the server holds nothing older); a `#msg_…` deep link walks older pages (`loadUntil`, capped at ten requests) and the wall's effect resumes when the mark arrives. Every loader runs through one queue, so a poll never interleaves with a page fetch. The "N marks left" pill and the page rail read the server's total (plus pending cards) — the whole wall is navigable before it is loaded — and an unloaded leaf holds its space with the skeleton ghosts, with an inline *Try again* if its fetch fails. **One announcement per batch.** An aria-live region announces the DOM's state after React commits, so N `setAnnouncement` calls in one effect collapse into one text change. The wall's effect now collects every unseen arrival first — walking the newest-first list, then reading oldest first so the newest is heard last, as it would have been one at a time — and writes the region exactly once with `arrivalAnnouncement` (`events.js`, beside the arrival event it accompanies): the one-message copy is unchanged, a batch reads *"3 new messages. Alice: …. Bob: …. Carol: …"*. The headline's replay event is dispatched once per batch too, rather than restarting the same animation N times. **A write confirmed after a poll already saw it.** The server stores a message before it answers, so the list can already hold the real id beside the pending card when the 201 lands. The swap now settles that INSIDE the functional updater, against the list as it actually is — the ref mirror is synced in a passive effect and can be a render behind a fetch continuation: the polled copy is removed first, then the pending card becomes the server's copy in its slot, and the same verdict decides the total (a polled copy was counted by that poll already). To make list and total move together, they now live in one state object (`{ list, total }`) behind two thin setters — every other call site is unchanged in shape. The mirror race on delete is closed the same way: a poll that ran while the DELETE was in flight still saw the message and revived the card (and its count); the 200 now takes both back instead of leaving a zombie until the next poll. **Tests.** Unit: the two races under a stubbed `fetch` with the write held on a deferred — a reload between send and 201 leaves exactly one card counted once, a plain 201 still counts once, and a reload between bin and 200 leaves nothing; `arrivalAnnouncement` (empty, single, a three-mark batch naming each author exactly once, the nameless-author fallback); the order and tie-break, the cursor codec (round trip; the shapes it must refuse), `parseLimit`, the poll merge in six scenarios, `appendOlder`; the driver contract gains paging (page walk, exact end, empty wall, same-instant ties across a page edge) and `getMessage`; a new route suite drives the real `GET` handler over the json driver with `@/auth` stubbed anonymous — page + total + cursor with the private reaction map stripped, a full cursor walk seeing every message exactly once, the limit clamp/fallback, and the `400` for a foreign cursor. E2E: the composer spec's list stub now honours `limit`/`cursor` and logs requests, proving a 20-mark wall is fetched as `16` then `8` across two flips (the rail reads *of 3* from the count before leaf 3 exists locally) and that a deep link to the oldest mark walks `16` then `50` to reach page 3. |

#### `/guestbook` — review fixes: the e2e script builds first, a forced Redis driver fails fast, the share copy names both providers, the bundle analyzer loads on demand, Playwright never adopts a stale server, presence heartbeats are rate-limited per IP, a malformed deep-link hash no longer crashes the wall, presence ids no longer depend on a secure-context API, the signature pad's point cap is derived from the server's byte cap, ink survives a pad resize, the dev rate limit can no longer be raced, the e2e server can no longer reach the live Redis, and the json driver serialises its writes

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Code review of the `/guestbook` branch — thirteen findings (five from the owner's review, eight from CodeRabbit), each reproduced against the code before it was touched. |
| **Files** | **new** `tests/unit/presenceRoute.test.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/deepLink.js`, `tests/unit/deepLink.test.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/anonId.js`, `tests/unit/anonId.test.js`; **changed** `src/components/guestbook/GuestbookWall.jsx`, `src/hooks/usePresence.js`, `src/hooks/useSignaturePad.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/signature.js`, `tests/unit/signature.test.js`, `package.json`, `next.config.mjs`, `playwright.config.mjs`, `src/lib/guestbook/store.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/jsonDriver.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/redisDriver.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/ratelimit.js`, `src/lib/guestbook/presence.js`, `src/app/api/guestbook/presence/route.js`, `tests/unit/ratelimit.test.js`, `tests/unit/store.test.js`, `src/app/(sub pages)/guestbook/layout.js`, `src/app/(sub pages)/guestbook/opengraph-image.js`, `src/app/api/guestbook/route.js`, `.env.example`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | **`npm run test:e2e` failed on a clean checkout.** `playwright.config.mjs` boots a PRODUCTION server (`next start` on port 3100) and its header — like the README — promised that the `test:e2e` script chains the build; the script was a bare `playwright test`, so with no production build output present (`.next.nosync` locally, per `distDir`) Next exited on "Could not find a production build" and every spec failed before running (CI never noticed: `ci.yml` runs lint + build, not the e2e suite). The script is now `npm run build && playwright test` — through `npm run build` rather than a bare `next build` so the Node-version guard in `scripts/next-cmd.mjs` still applies — which makes both existing claims true. **`GUESTBOOK_DRIVER=redis` without credentials handed the routes a null client.** `resolveDriver()` returned `redisDriver` unconditionally on the forced value, while `redisDriver.js` leaves its client `null` when neither `KV_REST_API_*` nor `UPSTASH_*` is set (and its own comment claimed the store only selects it when the env is present); the first `GET /api/guestbook` then died on `zrange` of null as a 500, taking the rate limiter's json fallback (which reads through the store) with it — `presence.js` and `ratelimit.js` were already guarded on `redisAvailable`; the store was the one gap. The forced path now throws at module load with an error naming the variables to set (never their values), deliberately NOT falling back to the file driver — on Vercel that fallback would accept messages and lose them on the next cold start while the operator believed they were in Redis. The `redisDriver.js` comment now states the real invariant, and a vitest case (module registry reset, credentials cleared, driver forced) asserts the import rejects with that message. **Share metadata said "signed through GitHub" on a page with two sign-in buttons.** The layout description — which `sectionMetadata` fans out to the page description, `og:description` and `twitter:description`, i.e. every search snippet and link unfurl — and the tagline baked into the build-time share-card PNG both predated the Google provider. Both now read "GitHub or Google", and the API route's header comment — which describes the security boundary — was brought back in line with the handlers on both verbs: POST accepts any signed-in session (GitHub or Google), and DELETE is own-or-admin (the stored author, or `GUESTBOOK_ADMIN`), not the admin-only rule the comment still described. Social scrapers cache cards aggressively, so X / LinkedIn / Slack may show the old image until re-scraped through their card validators. **`next.config.mjs` imported a devDependency statically.** `@next/bundle-analyzer` is dev-only, yet the config imported it at the top level — and `next start` evaluates the config too, so a production install pruned of devDependencies (`npm ci --omit=dev`, a Docker runtime stage) would die on a missing module before `ANALYZE` was ever read. Vercel deploys never tripped over it (the build installs devDependencies), which is how it went unnoticed. The analyzer is now `await import()`ed only when `ANALYZE=true` and is otherwise an identity wrapper, verified through Next's own config loader in both modes. **Playwright could silently adopt whatever was on port 3100.** `reuseExistingServer: !process.env.CI` is the documented idiom, but here the `webServer.env` block is load-bearing (json driver, fake `AUTH_SECRET`, `AUTH_TRUST_HOST`) and a reused process never receives it — and with the build now chained into `test:e2e`, "reuse" would have meant testing a stale `next start` against the build just produced. Reuse is now an explicit opt-in (`E2E_REUSE_SERVER=1`, for iterating on specs against a server you started yourself with the same env); by default an occupied port is a loud error, not a silent substitution — both paths verified against a throwaway listener on the port. **Anonymous presence heartbeats had no ceiling.** `POST /api/guestbook/presence` is unauthenticated by design (watching who is around is part of the read-only experience) and validated only the id's SHAPE — so every unique well-formed id became a ZADD, and one script looping over random ids could inflate "N here now" without bound while keeping Redis busy indefinitely. Heartbeats are now metered per client IP before anything is written: `PRESENCE_BEATS_PER_MINUTE` (30) on an `@upstash/ratelimit` sliding window under `guestbook:rl:presence:` — with the library's in-process cache of denied keys, so a flood costs Redis nothing after its first denial — and an in-memory sliding window in dev, mirroring presence.js's own fallback. Over budget is a 429 with `Retry-After` and the id is NOT registered, which pins an attacker's phantom presence at the budget per IP per minute; a legitimate tab beats 4×/min (plus one per return to the tab), so the budget fits a handful of tabs. Presence stays anonymous by construction: the limiter keys on a truncated SHA-256 of the address, never the address itself. The IP comes from `x-real-ip`, then the leftmost `x-forwarded-for` hop (both set by Vercel, which overwrites inbound values). Tests: the limiter's budget, honest retry time, per-client isolation, sliding behaviour and hashed key in `ratelimit.test.js`; and the endpoint test the finding asked for — a unique-id flood from one client through the real route handler: the budget accepted with the count climbing, the next refused with 429, `GET` confirming the refused id was never counted, another client still served, malformed ids rejected before touching the budget, and the proxy-header fallback. **A malformed deep-link hash took the whole wall down.** The `/guestbook#msg_…` effect called `decodeURIComponent` on the hash unguarded; `decodeURIComponent` throws `URIError` on an invalid percent sequence, so a link like `/guestbook#%zz` threw during commit and unmounted the wall — while the comment above it promised that a mangled link is a no-op. The decode now lives in a pure helper (`src/lib/guestbook/deepLink.js` → `messageIdFromHash`, the validate.js doctrine: small, window-free, testable) that returns an empty id on a failed decode, so the effect falls through to its existing no-op path. Unit-tested for the plain link, a percent-encoded one, the empty/bare hash, and three malformed sequences that must not throw. **Presence ids depended on a secure-context API.** `usePresence`'s `tabId()` called `crypto.randomUUID` in BOTH branches of its try/catch — but `randomUUID` is secure-context only, so over plain http on a LAN address (precisely how this dev server is viewed from a real phone, per the CSP work above) it is `undefined`: the try threw, the catch re-threw, and the effect took the whole wall down for a decorative counter. The id now comes from a new `src/lib/guestbook/anonId.js` → `randomAnonId()`, which never throws and degrades through three tiers (`randomUUID` → `getRandomValues`, which is not secure-context gated → time + `Math.random`, the contact form's idempotency-key idiom; presence only needs to dedupe tabs, not be unguessable). The same module owns `PRESENCE_ID_RE`, which the presence route now imports instead of its own copy, so a fallback-minted id can never be one the server rejects — and the unit test asserts every tier against that very regex, plus the never-throws guarantee with a crypto object whose methods are present but broken. Verified end to end in headless Chromium against the dev server with `Crypto.prototype.randomUUID` deleted before load: the wall renders, no page error, and the heartbeat POST carries a server-valid id. **The signature pad could draw what the route refuses.** The pad's 160-point budget was justified by "~24 bytes per smoothed point", but the serialiser clamped BOTH axes to 100 (the space is 100×40), so a stroke captured and dragged off the bottom-right corner emitted five-character numerals on every coordinate: 26 bytes per smoothed point, 28 per dot — a continuous 160-point stroke measured 4,135 bytes against the route's 4,096 cap, and the visitor's signature came back "Invalid signature" after the UI had accepted every stroke. The serialiser now lives in `signature.js` as a pure `strokesToPath()` beside the validator it must agree with, clamps each axis to its own viewbox bound (a `y` of 100 in a 40-tall space was outside the glyph as well as a byte wider), and the point cap is exported from the same module as `MAX_SIGNATURE_POINTS` = 150 — DERIVED: the costliest point is a far-corner dot at 26 bytes and two commands, so 150 points is at most 3,900 bytes / 300 commands under both caps (157 was the exact ceiling; CodeRabbit's 100 would have thrown away fidelity for nothing). The hook is now a thin wrapper computing the pad's scale. Tests pin the derivation itself, both worst cases at the cap (all dots, one continuous stroke) passing the real validator, the old 160 failing it, the per-axis clamp, a scaled real-pad stroke's exact output, and the empty case. **A pad resize clipped or displaced the ink — and changed the signature.** Strokes are recorded in css px of the box they were drawn in, and the pad's `fit()` (its ResizeObserver callback) re-rasterised the bitmap and replayed them unchanged, so a viewport resize, a phone rotating, or the on-screen keyboard reflowing the composer clipped ink the pad had shrunk away from or left it huddled top-left when the pad grew — and `toPathString`, which normalises against the CURRENT box, then shipped a different glyph from the one the visitor saw. `fit()` now remembers the last real css box and, when it changes, rescales every stored stroke (and a mid-flight point, if a stroke is down) from the old box to the new one through pure `rescaleStrokes`/`rescalePoint` helpers in `signature.js` before redrawing: same fraction of the pad per point, which is exactly what the normalised path encodes, so the serialisation is invariant across the resize. A hidden pad's 0×0 is not treated as a size, so a panel toggled closed and reopened at a new width still scales from the box the ink was truly laid out in. Tests: per-axis ratios, the serialisation invariant across three non-uniform resizes, and the same-box / hidden-pad no-ops. **The json-driver rate limit could be raced.** The Redis limiter consumes the posting slot atomically at check time, but the json path only READ the stored messages — which change on insert — so two concurrent POSTs by one user could both pass `checkRateLimit` before either `addMessage` ran, landing two messages inside the five-minute window. An admitted check now also RESERVES the slot in a module-scoped map, and the check-and-reserve runs in one synchronous section after the file read (no `await` between the read resolving and the set); on a single Node process — the only shape the json driver is coherent in — that is atomic, so the second of two concurrent posts always sees the first's reservation. The stored post and the reservation combine by max (a reservation never hides a newer stored post), reservations age out with the window, and — like the Redis limiter — an admission whose insert then fails still holds the slot (fail closed). The same review's other option, refusing the json driver for public deployments, is taken as a WARNING rather than a throw — `store.js` logs once when the json driver finds itself under `NODE_ENV=production` — because the e2e suite legitimately boots a production server on the json driver. The warning is gated to SERVING: `next build` evaluates route modules under `NODE_ENV=production` too (once per worker — the e2e clone's credential-free build printed it three times), but nothing is served at build time, so `NEXT_PHASE === 'phase-production-build'` keeps quiet. Tests: three concurrent checks for one user admit exactly one (the other two refused with the full-window retry), a reservation expires with the window, a reservation never masks a newer stored post; the pre-existing cases now use distinct usernames because an admission is, correctly, a post for the purposes of the next check. Follow-up on review: every case in `ratelimit.test.js` (posting AND presence) now builds its own state — its own user or address, its own admissions — instead of leaning on a neighbour's, verified by running each test alone by name and the whole file in shuffled order under three seeds. **The "hermetic" e2e suite could write to the live presence set.** Playwright merges `webServer.env` over the parent process environment, and `next start` loads `.env.local` besides — either can carry the real Upstash credentials — while the presence path keys on `redisAvailable` alone by design (it deliberately ignores `GUESTBOOK_DRIVER`), so the smoke spec's heartbeat, which unlike the composer spec's is not stubbed, landed in production's `guestbook:presence` set (and its limiter key) on any machine with credentials, this one included. The config now sets all four credential names (`KV_REST_API_*`, `UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_*`) to the empty string in the spawned server's env: a defined-but-empty variable overrides the shell AND blocks `.env.local` — verified against this repo's real `.env.local`: with only the URL emptied the token still loaded while the URL stayed empty, because Next never overwrites a variable that is already defined — and `redisDriver` reads empty as unconfigured, so presence and its limiter run in memory for the duration of the run. Full build + suite re-run in an isolated clone with the emptied env: all five specs pass. **The json driver's own read-modify-write could lose posts.** Independently of the rate-limit race above: `addMessage`, `deleteMessage` and `setReaction` each read the whole array, mutated it and wrote it back with nothing serialising them, so two users posting at once could both read the same array and the later write silently dropped the earlier post — the same for a delete undone or a reaction vanished by a concurrent write — and `writeAll` truncated the file in place, so a reader could see a torn half-written JSON. Every mutation now runs through one in-process promise queue (strictly one at a time; a rejected task rejects its caller but never wedges the tasks behind it), and `writeAll` lands the contents in a uniquely named sibling temp file and `rename()`s it over the real one — atomic on POSIX, a replace on Windows — cleaning up after a failed attempt. Reads (`getMessages`, `getReactions`) stay lock-free for exactly that reason: they see the old file or the new one, never a torn one. Tests: 40 concurrent posts all survive with distinct ids, an interleaved add/react/delete mix lands in one consistent end state, no temp files remain after concurrent writes, and a mutation forced to fail (data path under a FILE) rejects, leaves no debris, and the next mutation succeeds. |

#### `/journey` — a keyboard-driven atlas jump now shows where focus landed

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | CodeRabbit review of the branch (the eighth finding in this pass; the other seven are the `/guestbook` table above). The `jn-<id>` node that both the overlap atlas's bars and the ⌘K palette hand focus to carried `outline-none` with no replacement, so a keyboard user who activated a bar had their focus moved somewhere invisible — and the next Tab set off from a place they could not see. |
| **Files** | `src/components/journey/TimelineNode.jsx` |
| **Details** | The node keeps `tabIndex -1` (it is handed focus, never tabbed onto) and still drops the browser's default outline on itself — a 2px rectangle around a full-width, padded row would have been the wrong shape — but becomes a named group (`group/jn`) whose focus-visible state is expressed on the CARD: the site's own idiom, a 2px `#ff6d05` outline offset 2px, following the card's `rounded-xl` and independent of `custom-bg-abt`'s gold box-shadow (a ring would have composed into the same `box-shadow` property). `:focus-visible` rather than `:focus` is the point: a jump made from the keyboard (Enter on a bar, or a palette action) inherits the initiator's focus-visible state and lights the card, while a mouse click on the same bar — which never matches `:focus-visible` — moves focus just as before with nothing drawn. Verified in headless Chromium against the dev server: activeElement lands on the `jn-` node in both cases; the card's computed outline is solid ember after a keyboard activation and `none` after a pointer one. |

#### CI — the unit and e2e suites now gate pull requests

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Owner review of the branch: `ci.yml` still ran lint and build only, so none of the regressions pinned by the new vitest and Playwright suites — the driver races, the rate-limit reservation, the presence flood, the signature budget, the deep-link decode, the palette exit, the pad resize — gated a pull request; they ran only when someone remembered to run them locally. |
| **Files** | `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `README.md` |
| **Details** | Two steps join the existing job: **Unit tests** (`npm test`) right after lint, because it is fast and most regressions land there first; and an **E2E gate** after the build — `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium`, then `npx playwright test --reporter=list,html`. Playwright runs directly rather than via `npm run test:e2e` on purpose: that script chains `npm run build` so a clean local checkout works, but the job's Build step has already produced the output `next start` boots from, and building twice would double the job for nothing. The server step carries the same non-secret stubs the build got plus placeholder OAuth client ids so Auth.js boots (the specs only ever read the unauthenticated session, or stub it at the network layer); nothing in the job touches a live service — the config empties the Redis credential names and `reuseExistingServer` is off unless opted into. On an e2e failure the HTML report is uploaded as an artifact (7-day retention), and the job summary gains Unit and E2E rows beside Lint and Build. The job's id and display name are deliberately unchanged so any branch-protection rule naming "Lint & Build" keeps working. Verified by emulating the job in a clone stripped of every `.env*.local`: the build with the workflow's exact env compiled all 67 pages, and Playwright with the e2e step's exact env passed 5/5 against that build and produced the HTML report the artifact step uploads. |

#### ⌘K command palette — the hotkey now closes through the same path as Esc

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | CodeRabbit review of the branch (the ninth finding in this pass). Pressing ⌘K / Ctrl+K while the palette was open toggled `open` inside a functional state updater and nothing else — the typed query and the arrow selection survived into the next open, and focus was never handed back to the element that had it before the palette took it. Esc, by contrast, went through `close()` and did all three. |
| **Files** | `src/hooks/useCommandPalette.js`; **new** `tests/unit/useCommandPalette.test.js` |
| **Details** | `close()` is now declared first and documented as the palette's ONE exit; the hotkey effect reads `open` from its closure (re-subscribing the window listener on each toggle, which is trivially cheap) so its closing branch calls `close()` and its opening branch alone records the focus-restore target. The shared component (`src/components/commandPalette`) is untouched — it only renders, and focuses its input on open. A jsdom hook test pins the contract from three directions: a focused button, ⌘K opens, the palette's input takes focus, a query that keeps several results plus an arrow selection are set, ⌘K again → closed, query empty, selection at the top, focus back on the button; Esc from the same setup lands in the identical state; and the next ⌘K opens clean and re-records whichever element has focus by then. |

#### The orbital-nav tip stops rendering where the orbit itself does not exist

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | [#105](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/105) follow-up, owner-reported: on a phone the tip appeared over the two-column layout — a message about the orbital navigation on the one layout that never shows an orbit |
| **Files** | `src/components/navigation/OrbitTip.jsx` |
| **Details** | Below 480px the nav collapses to two fixed columns (`navigation/index.jsx`), and the first pass handled that by RE-WORDING the tip ("tap a button on either side to explore") — but eight visible, labelled buttons need no coach mark, and the card reads as chrome about a nav bar the phone never shows. The tip now does not render there at all. The gate sits on the RENDER (`visible = status === 'visible' && !isColumns`), not on the state machine: the show timer still arms behind the scenes, so a phone rotating into a ≥480px landscape — where the ring IS the nav, un-discovered — gains the tip live, and rotating back loses it, all off the existing `(max-width: 479.98px)` media-query subscription with no timer re-runs. The interaction auto-dismissal is gated the same way: a column tap used to `persistDismissed()`, permanently burning the one showing a later desktop visit is owed, on the strength of an interaction that proves nothing about the ring — `interacted` now dismisses only when the orbit layout is live. The columns wording branch is deleted as dead code (`mode` is back to the hover/touch pair). Verified against the dev server via Chrome DevTools mobile emulation with the dismissal key cleared: at 390×844 (touch, columns) the `role="status"` region stays empty 12s after load — no tip, ever; at 768×1024 (touch, orbit) the tip still appears with the touch telling ("Slide up and down on the laptop — or drag the ring"). **Then made airtight on review** (a CodeRabbit finding, verified): the `!isColumns` gate only DEFERRED the burn — the page's `interacted` latch is never cleared, so a column tap below 480px followed by a rotation into ≥480px re-ran the dismissal effect against the stale latch and persisted the dismissal at the exact moment the un-discovered ring appeared, defeating the rotation-gains-the-tip contract this same entry promises. The signal is now ring-only at the SOURCE: the column wrappers no longer raise `onUserInteract` at all (wheel/drag/scrub/arrow keys — inputs that only exist in the ring layout — are the only producers left), and the tip's `!isColumns` guard stays as the statement of that contract should a columns-mode producer ever come back. And the window-level Esc listener is gated on the RENDERED `visible`, not `status` alone — switching to columns hides the tip but leaves `status === 'visible'`, so an Esc pressed for something else while the tip was hidden would persist the dismissal of a card nobody saw |

#### The dev server rendered as page-tall grey voids on any real phone

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | Owner-reported alongside the tip bug: "each page has a grey one page worth of space under each page" on a mobile phone |
| **Files** | `next.config.mjs` |
| **Details** | The report reproduced in exactly one environment: the DEV server viewed over plain `http://` in real WebKit — the iOS Simulator's Safari rendered every route as a near-empty white/grey page with a page-tall void under a line of unstyled links. The cause is the CSP's `upgrade-insecure-requests` directive: real WebKit (iOS Safari, the Simulator) honours it even for `http://localhost` and LAN addresses, upgrading every stylesheet/script/image request to an `https://…:3000` origin that does not answer, so the HTML lands and its assets all fail — and since framer-motion SSR inlines `opacity:0` on entrance-gated blocks, most of each page's markup is invisible even unstyled, leaving viewport-tall blank runs. Desktop Chrome exempts localhost from the upgrade, which is why the same server always looked fine from the editor. The directive now ships in production only (`NODE_ENV === 'production'`), where every request is https anyway and it keeps doing its real job; the production header string is byte-identical. Ruled out on the way, all clean: every route under Chrome DevTools mobile+touch emulation at 390×844 and 375×667 (dev AND production builds), under emulated `prefers-reduced-motion`, in the iOS Simulator against the fixed dev server (footer reveal fires, pages end exactly at the wordmark), and live production `ma.codes` in both engines — the deployed site never had the bug |

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (review note: `md:text-md` is not a Tailwind class in this repo, so it never took effect and only added confusion to the tooltip styling) |
| **Files** | `src/components/navigation/NavButton.jsx` · `src/components/navigation/index.jsx` |
| **Details** | The hover label's class list carried `text-sm md:text-md` — but Tailwind's default `fontSize` scale has no `md` step (`sm` hands over to `base`), and `tailwind.config.js` extends `fontFamily` and `colors` only, so the JIT compiler has never emitted a rule for it: the label renders `text-sm` at every width, and the phantom class only read as a breakpoint step that does not exist. That misdirection had a real cost on record — `LABEL_DROP` in `navigation/index.jsx`, the constant the label-flip geometry hangs off, is derived from the label's CSS on the stated premise that no `sm:`/`md:` step applies, and its comment had to call out the dead class by name to make that derivation trustworthy. The class is removed and the comment's parenthetical retired with it. Behaviour is identical by construction — a rule that was never in the sheet cannot stop applying — and the 23px drop the flip arithmetic reserves stays correct at every width |

#### A late `fonts.ready` could measure the hero into a dead tree

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (review note: `document.fonts.ready` resolves asynchronously and can fire after a client-side navigation away from `/`; the ResizeObserver and resize listener are removed in cleanup, but a promise cannot be unsubscribed) |
| **Files** | `src/app/page.js` |
| **Details** | The orbit's own webfont re-measure (`navigation/index.jsx`) has always run behind a `cancelled` flag; the homepage's twin — added for the same reason, and saying so in its comment — did not, so on a navigation racing the webfont the surviving promise called `measureBase` into an unmounted tree. React 18 makes that state write a silent no-op rather than a leak, so this is hygiene rather than a visible defect — but it is also the pattern's one real trap left open: the callback reads refs, and any future DOM read added to it would throw on the nulls an unmounted tree hands back. All three signals (observer, resize, `fonts.ready`) now route through one guarded wrapper so they age the same way, and the cleanup flips the flag first. The other two `fonts.ready` review reports — that the non-optional `.catch()` after `?.then()` throws when `document.fonts` is missing — were declined with the semantics demonstrated: an optional chain short-circuits the ENTIRE remainder of the chain, so `a?.b?.then(f).catch(g)` is `undefined`, not a `TypeError`; only a parenthesized `(a?.b?.then(f)).catch(g)` throws, and no site in the repo writes that form (verified across all seven `fonts.ready` call sites). ESLint clean |

#### The hover label was hover-only — a sighted keyboard user tabbing the ring got no visible label

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (review note: the label appears only on `group-hover`; the link's `aria-label` covers assistive technology, but a sighted keyboard user tabbing to a button saw nothing) |
| **Files** | `src/components/navigation/NavButton.jsx` · `README.md` |
| **Details** | `group-focus-visible:opacity-100` alone would have been the textbook fix, and here it would have been a wrong one: a visible label is only flip-stable because the ring is FROZEN while it shows — `labelAbove` re-evaluates as the button's `y` changes, so a label revealed on a still-rotating ring flips sides mid-display, rides behind the laptop on the far half of each lap, and can run off the page foot: exactly the modes the hover pause exists to prevent, and the reason the flip logic's own comment leans on "hovering already pauses the rotation". So focus pauses the lap through the same `setHovered` gate hover uses (the rAF loop keeps ticking and simply stops advancing the angle, so there is nothing extra to resume) and blur releases it, while the REVEAL stays on `:focus-visible` so a mouse click cannot flash a label — after a click the pointer is on the button and hover holds the pause anyway, and Safari never focuses links on click at all. One shared boolean means a pointer brushing a second button can briefly resume the ring under a keyboard-focused label; accepted and recorded in place — the label tracks its own button and refreezes the moment the pointer leaves. The focus handler keeps the `/qualifications` intent-warming it already carried, and the xs-mobile column branch is untouched (it has no visible label and no rotation to pause). Tailwind ^3.3 composes `group-focus-visible` natively. ESLint clean |

#### Registering `--ring-size` armed its `0px` initial in the ladder's fractional gaps

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (review note: the rungs are exclusive ranges — `≤639.98px`, `640–767.98px`, `≥768px` — so a fractional-zoom width inside a 0.02px complement resolves the registered property to its `initial-value: 0px` and collapses all three rings) |
| **Files** | `src/app/globals.css` |
| **Details** | The `@property` registration that fixed the `getComputedStyle` read-back quietly changed what a cascade miss means. Unregistered, a width inside one of the rung complements (639.98–640 or 767.98–768, reachable under fractional zoom / DPR scaling) left `var(--ring-size)` unset and made the `width`/`height` declarations invalid at computed-value time — `auto`, ugly but sized. Registered, the same miss substitutes the declared initial, and `calc(0px * var(--ring-fit))` is three rings at zero size. The guard is a base rung written **one specificity step below the ladder**: bare `.borderline`/`.borderline2`/`.borderline3` (0,1,0) against the rungs' `div.`-qualified (0,1,1), so it loses to every rung wherever one matches — independent of source order, the lever this file's own comments document as untrustworthy against the ramp — and surfaces only where none does. It carries the phone values (150/220/320): the lower gap adjoins the phone rung, and 0.02px of half-size rings at 767.99px beats 0.02px of none. The initial stays `0px` deliberately — it can no longer render on these elements, and a made-up non-zero length would only disguise a future regression as a wrong size instead of an obvious one. Engines without `@property` gain the same guard (the gap now yields the base instead of an invalid declaration). Sheet re-parsed clean; all 15 `--ring-size` declarations verified in place, the twelve rungs media-scoped and qualified, the three base rules bare and unconditioned |

#### The orbit read as passing under the laptop, the laptop sat undersized on tablets and big desktops, and at 1440px the rings had shrunk until the laptop swallowed them

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | [#93](https://github.com/MA1002643/theabdullahfolio/issues/93) continuation (owner report, three parts: "on some of the screen width it gives an impression that vertically the navigation bar is orbiting under the laptop … raise the navigation bar 15% vertically against the laptop"; "on some of the screen widths the size of the laptop is small which doesn't look suitable on the overall page"; "at 1,440 pixels the size of the rings compared to the size of the laptop is too small — I can only see the last and a portion of the second to last ring") |
| **Files** | `src/components/navigation/index.jsx` · `src/components/navigation/NavButton.jsx` · `src/app/page.js` · `src/app/globals.css` · `README.md` |
| **Details** | **All three reports are one geometry, measured before touching anything (18 viewports, 480-1920 wide, 390-1112 tall).** (1) The orbit's centre was "the hero row's centre, dropped up to `ORBIT_DROP = 64`" — a proxy for the laptop's base dating from when the ground rings were positioned the same way, and it landed anywhere from **81px above the base (1024x640) to 27px below it (768x900)**; at the 768-834 tablet widths the centre sat **46% of the laptop's height below the laptop's own centre**, so with the ellipse only 0.204 as tall as it is wide every button orbited the bottom quarter of a 245px laptop — the reported "orbiting under" impression. (2) The `md` band pinned the laptop at a flat `22rem`: **352px on an 834x1112 iPad with 766px of hero row**, and 480px sat in acreage at 1728+. Worse, the ladder refactor that moved `--laptop-w` into the base `img.laptop` rule had **silently killed the 640-767 dead-band ramp** — same 0,1,1 specificity, later in the file, so the band ran at 70% (`490px at 700, 537 at 767`) and fell **185px in one pixel of width at 768**: the exact cascade fault the ring-size ladder's bounding comment warns about, live on the one property that was not bounded. (3) At 1440x790 — a 13" MBP in full-screen Chrome — the laptop's `max-height: 100%` let it fill the row to **25px above the fold**, leaving the ring fit 58px of room below the base: rings shrunk to **0.60, a 359px outer ring UNDER a 466px laptop**, the inner two swallowed whole. The laptop had given up 3% while the rings paid 40%. **Fixes, in the same order.** The orbit now anchors to the laptop's **measured base** — the same `--lap-base-y` the ground rings sit on, handed down as props with the laptop's rendered height — and holds its centre **`ORBIT_RAISE = 0.15` of the laptop's height above that base**, the owner-specified raise, at every viewport; the drop is still clamped to what the row affords (two-sided now, since the anchor can sit above the row's centre), the fallback frame before measurement keeps the old pose, and the depth sort still reads the un-dropped `yOrbit`. Measured after: **centre at base − 15% ± 0 at all 14 orbital viewports** (was −81..+27 scatter), the far arc crossing the laptop's midriff instead of its feet. The laptop's `md` rung becomes a **432 → 480 ramp arriving exactly on `lg` at 1024** (dead band re-landed on 432: an 18px descent instead of a 185px cliff, with the 70% rung scoped to `max-width: 639.98px` where it belongs), and an **xl rung ramps 480 → 512 over 1536-1728** with the rings (600 → 640 etc.) and the orbit's width cap (`maxSemiAxis`, 425 → 453 = the same 512/480) running the identical ramp so the three grow together. Tablets: **352 → 432 at 768x900, 352 → 444 at 834x1112 (+26%)**. And the laptop's height bound gains a second operand — `max-height: min(100%, calc(75% + 35px))` of the stage — which is the even split solved, not styled: scale the laptop and the rings down TOGETHER in the `lg` proportion as the row shortens, using the same perspective arithmetic the ring fit runs (the ring's reach relaxes faster than its size, so the trade is far from linear). The line binds at exactly the laptop's natural height on a stage that already fits (nothing moves at 1440x900, 1728x1000, or any phone — their ladder heights sit far under it), and below that holds the rendered outer ring at **~1.2x the laptop's width** against the full composition's 1.25. At the reported viewport: **laptop 466 → 400, outer ring 359 → 488 (fit 0.60 → 0.81), all three ripples closing around the art**; same shape at 1024x768 (342 → 473), 1280x800 (364 → 496), 1366x640 (270 → 349). **Two more defects surfaced by the resize sweep, both fixed.** `--ring-size` was read back with `getComputedStyle` to learn the unfitted ladder — but an unregistered custom property computes to its raw token stream, so every `calc()`/`clamp()` rung (the whole 640-767 ramp, the new xl ramp) came back unparseable and the measurement bailed **holding the previous viewport's fit**: cold loads in the band never shrank the rings at all, and a live resize into it kept the old factor (measured 0.813 carried from 1440x790 into 700x900, where 1 is correct). `@property --ring-size { syntax: '<length>' }` makes the computed value a resolved px length at every width; engines without `@property` keep today's bail-out, degraded never broken. And the contact shadow's px steps diverged from a laptop whose rendered size is `min(width ladder, height cap)` — 420px of shadow under the 400px laptop at 1440x790 — so from 640 up it is now a fixed **0.875 ratio of the laptop's measured width** (`--lap-w`, published off the same read as the base; verified 0.876 live), replacing the dead-band shadow ramp and the `sm:`/`md:`/`lg:` utilities in one move. The orbit also gains a `ResizeObserver` on its own wrapper — the row changes height with no window resize when the maintenance header's data lands or the headline re-flows, and only `resize` was listened to — re-bound across the sub-480 layout crossing (which also retires the separate crossing-up re-measure effect). **Verified end to end: the same 18-viewport sweep re-run after** — outer-ring-to-laptop ratio 1.19-1.25 at every crunched viewport (was 0.71-0.85 wherever the fit bound), orbit centre at base − 15% everywhere the row affords it, laptop monotonic across 640/768/1024/1536 boundaries (450 → 432 → 480 → 512, no step over 1px/px), `scrollHeight == viewport` at all 15 heights that fit (no new scrollbar anywhere) and the 844x390 landscape phone still scrolling to its designed 580 floor with the lowest button 12px inside it; live-resize continuity exercised 1440x790 → 700x900 → 400x800 → 1024x768 → 1440x790 (fit correct at each step, column layout in and out, no hysteresis); hover label on the lowest button lands 7px inside the fold at 1440x790; ESLint clean, production build clean (run in an APFS clone so the live dev server's `.next` stayed untouched), no error overlay, and the only console output on the route is the pre-existing `/api/spotify` 502. **An adversarial multi-agent review of the finished diff then confirmed two more defects, both fixed.** The `max-height: 740px` cap block's inner-ring fractions (0.5833/0.8333 of `--hero-cap`) still encoded the retired 280/400 md rungs, so under 740px of height the inner two ripples were clamped ~4-21px below the shared `--ring-fit` factor while the outer ring's 1.25 matched its rung exactly — the three rings shrinking NON-uniformly, and the inner one snapping ~6px at the 741→740 boundary the block's header promises is seamless (measured live at 768x740, fit 0.836) — retuned to 0.7083/0.9583 (= 340/480, 460/480) so the block is inert at exactly 740 again. And the flipped hover label's `bottom-full mb-1` was not the "exact reflection" of `top-full -mt-1` its comment claimed: with `bottom: 100%` a POSITIVE margin-bottom pushes the label a further 4px up, so flipped tooltips sat 8px farther from their glyph than un-flipped ones — now `-mb-1`, landing the text 1px inside the circle's edge on both sides as documented. The same review confirmed five stale comments (page.js's two "at the same values" ladder claims, the dead-band "450->352" reference, the cap block's fraction table, and the max-height derivation overstating its 0.75x+35 line as "the line through the solved points" when it is a chosen balance between the fully-proportional 0.66x+52 and the old 100%) — all rewritten to match the code |

#### The ground rings were never in anyone's fit arithmetic, and on a 13" MacBook Pro the outermost one ran under the bottom edge

| | |
|:--|:--|
| **Ref** | — (owner report from the machine itself: "MacBook Pro M1 13inch 2020 full screen chrome and on that the rings are still going off the screen, from the b[ottom] ... even the outer ring full circle should be fully visible at all times") |
| **Files** | `src/app/page.js` · `src/app/globals.css` · `README.md` |
| **Details** | **The orbit was taught to stay on the page. The GROUND rings never were, and they reach further down than anything else in the hero.** Measured at 1440x790 — a 13" MBP in full-screen Chrome — the outermost ripple ran **80px past the foot of the page** and the middle one 14px, while the nav buttons cleared it by 16 and the laptop by 25. Two blind spots put it there: the ripple rings are decorative, so they sat outside the orbit's fit arithmetic by construction, and the `max-height: 740px` cap block that would otherwise have shrunk them **switches on below where this happens** — 790 is above that boundary, so the rings sit at their full `lg` 600px with nothing capping them at all. **The rings now shrink until they close, and the page does not grow.** A first pass lengthened the page instead and it was the wrong answer, for a reason worth recording: making the missing arc scrollable-to leaves the FIRST view — the one anybody actually looks at — cut exactly as before, so it added a scrollbar to every laptop and fixed nothing visible. That pass was chosen on a bad number, too. Dividing the available room by the 600px ring's own ratio (152.8 / 600 = 0.255) says a 74px gap fits a 290px ring, which made shrinking look ruinous; but the ring's reach is **not proportional to its size** — it is projected through `perspective(600px) rotateX(80deg)` and the divisor relaxes as the ring shrinks, so solving properly gives **418px**, and the straight-line estimate under-rated the achievable ring by 30%. So the fit multiplies the ring's BOX rather than its painted result, and that distinction is worth 22 points of ring on its own: at 1440x790 fitting by box needs 60% to close where fitting by painted size needs 38% for identical clearance. One factor drives all three rings so they keep their spacing — capping each against the same room would pull the outer two onto one circle, and they ripple in phase, so three rings would read as two.
